Global wellposedness of general nonlinear evolution equations for distributions on the Fourier half space Thanks: The first author (K. N.) was supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant Numbers JP20H01814 and JP22H01132, the second author (B. W.) was supported in part by NSFC Grant Number 12171007.
Kenji Nakanishi, Baoxiang Wang

Abstract
. The Cauchy problem is studied for very general systems of evolution equations, where the time derivative of solution is written by Fourier multipliers in space and analytic nonlinearity, with no other structural requirement. We construct a function space for the Fourier transform embedded in the space of distributions, and establish the global wellposedness with no size restriction. The major restriction on the initial data is that the Fourier transform is supported on the half space, decaying at the boundary in the sense of measure. We also require uniform integrability for the orthogonal directions in the distribution sense, but no other condition. In particular, the initial data may be much more rough than the tempered distributions, and may grow polynomially at the spatial infinity. A simpler argument is also presented for the solutions locally integrable in the frequency. When the Fourier support is slightly more restricted to a conical region, the generality of equations is extremely wide, including those that are even locally illposed in the standard function spaces, such as the backward heat equations, as well as those with infinite derivatives and beyond the natural boundary of the analytic nonlinearity. As more classical examples, our results may be applied to the incompressible and compressible Navier-Stokes and Euler equations, the nonlinear diffusion and wave equations, and so on. The major drawback of the Fourier support restriction is that the solutions cannot be real valued.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35A02, 35B60, 35F55, 35G55, 35K55, 35L60, 35L70, 35Q30, 35Q31, 35Q55, 46F05, 46F10

1.
Introduction

We consider general systems of nonlinear evolution equations in the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=L(D)u+N(D)\mathscr{H}(M(D)u),\ \ u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),

(1.1)

for the unknown function
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}
, where
L(D)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\hat{L}(\xi)\mathcal{F}
,
M(D)
and
N(D)
are given matrix-valued Fourier multipliers, defined with the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the symbols
\hat{L}(\xi)
,
\hat{M}(\xi)
,
\hat{N}(\xi)
of matrix size
n_{1}\times n_{1}
,
n_{2}\times n_{1}
,
n_{1}\times n_{3}
respectively,
for some
d,n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
.
\mathscr{H}
is a given analytic function defined on some neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{2}}
with values in
\mathbb{C}^{n_{3}}
, satisfying
\mathscr{H}(z)=o(z)
as
z\to 0
.

Each component of
L,M,N
with smooth symbol may be regarded as a linear and continuous map on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, namely the Fourier (inverse) image of the space of Schwartz distributions
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, defined as the dual space of
\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(for the precise definition, see
[
13
]
where it is denoted by
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
,
[
16
, Chapter II, Section 1.4]
where it is denoted by
Z^{\prime}
, or the end of this section).

The goal of this paper is to establish global wellposedness of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
) for such a wide class of equations in a certain function space embedded in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with no size restriction, but restricting the Fourier support to the half space
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. Such a kind of Fourier support conditions have definite meanings for the solutions of certain physical models. For instance, the solutions of the Constantin–Lax–Majda equation can be reduced to those of the complex heat equation which are supported in the Fourier half space, cf.
[
8
]
.
The form of equations (
1.1
) covers major nonlinear evolution equations, such as the Euler equations and the Navier-Stokes equations, both in the incompressible case and of the compressible models, and many other evolutional PDEs of nonlinear hyperbolic, parabolic, and dispersive types, though the complex analyticity of the nonlinear part
\mathscr{H}
may well require some extension or reformulation of the equations.

The advantage of the Fourier support restriction is to make sense of convolution between two distributions in the Fourier space, corresponding to the multiplication in the physical space.
Without that restriction, it would be highly non-trivial even to make sense of the equations in such generality, and of course, one should not expect the global wellposedness in any way compatible with the standard theories.
Nevertheless, there have been many attempts for general existence of weak solutions to nonlinear PDEs. In particular, there is an extensive literature of studies based on algebras of generalized functions, those introduced by Colombeau
[
9
]
and those by Rosinger
[
31
]
.
Without trying to be exhaustive, we just mention two results for arbitrary types of equations: the global Cauchy-Kovalevskaya theory for analytic equations and data
[
31
, Chapter 2]
, and local existence for general equations and data
[
10
]
.
Their settings are more general than ours, as the equations may contain variable coefficients and the initial data may contain arbitrary frequencies, but they also require considerable departure from the linear distribution theory, including the meanings of the generalized functions, the derivatives and the equations.

This paper is a generalization of the previous ones by the second author and others, where unique global existence of solutions was proven for the Navier-Stokes equations
[
14
]
, the complex-valued heat equations
[
8
]
, the nonlinear Schrödinger equations
[
7
]
and the nonlinear Klein-Gordon equation
[
34
]
, in very rough function spaces defined by negative exponential weight in the Fourier space, into which all the standard Sobolev spaces are continuously embedded.
Roughly speaking, besides the time decaying behavior and the lower regularity in Sobolev spaces, the results in
[
14
,
8
,
7
,
34
]
may be summarized as follows: If the initial data belongs to the negative exponential Sobolev (or modulation) space and the Fourier transform is supported in the first octant
[0,\infty)^{d}
, then those equations have unique global solutions in similar function spaces with less regularity.

The generalization in this paper is threefold. First, the equations are very general and not specialized, with no structural condition than those mentioned above (except some regularity and boundedness of the symbols
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
).
Second, the function space for the solutions is much wider.
In the previous papers, the Fourier transform was locally
L^{2}
and growing at most exponentially.
In this paper, it may be a distribution of arbitrary growth.
In particular, the solutions in this paper may decay, grow, or be periodic in space, while the previous ones decay in average sense.
Another benefit of the larger space is the wellposedness: solutions stay in the same space as the initial data, and are continuous for all time.
That was not the case in the previous papers, due to the regularity loss in time.
Third, the Fourier support is on the bigger set, namely the half space instead of the octant.
We will also consider subsets of cone shape with possibly growing aperture, for which the conditions on
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
become much less restrictive, and the octant is a special case.

Since the space
\mathcal{X}
is rather complicated, we will also consider smaller function spaces where the Fourier transform is locally integrable, with a much simpler proof with more explicit information on the solutions.

1.1.
Global wellposedness results

Now let us describe the main results in this paper about the global wellposedness for (
1.1
).
For any
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote

\begin{split}\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v}):=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi<a\},\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}_{(a,b)}(\mathrm{v}):=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid a<\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi<b\},\end{split}

(1.2)

and similarly define
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq a}(\mathrm{v})
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq a}(\mathrm{v})
, etc.
One may fix
\mathrm{v}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
without losing generality.
The function space
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined, to be used for the Fourier transform of solutions, as the set of distributions

F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the following two properties
(X-i)
–
(X-ii)
:

(X-i) Decay at the boundary as measure:

There exist
a\in(0,\infty)
and a complex Borel measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that
F
and
\mathfrak{m}
coincide when restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v})
, and the total variation
|\mathfrak{m}|
satisfies
|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq 0}(\mathrm{v}))=0
. (It implies
\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
.)

More concretely, there exist
a\in(0,\infty)
and finite Borel measures
\mathfrak{m}_{k}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq 0}(\mathrm{v}))=0
for
k=0,1,2,3
, such that for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v})
,

\begin{split}F(\varphi)=\sum_{k=0}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}i^{k}\varphi(x)d\mathfrak{m}_{k}(x).\end{split}

(1.3)

(X-ii) Integrability for the orthogonal directions as distribution:

For each
a\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
F|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v})}\in W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v}))
, namely the
L^{1}
-based Sobolev space of the negative regularity.

More concretely, for each
a\in(0,\infty)
, there exist finite subsets
A\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
and

\{f_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v})
,

\begin{split}F(\varphi)=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi(x)f_{\alpha}(x)dx.\end{split}

(1.4)

The space
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})
is equipped with a locally convex Hausdorff topology such that
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is continuous. See Section
2
for the definition and the properties of
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})
(and Lemma
2.4
for consistency with the above description).
Examples of
u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})
may be found in (
8.20
).

The condition
(X-i)
implies that
u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can not be real-valued unless it is
0
, because
u_{0}
is real-valued if and only if
\mathcal{F}u_{0}(-\xi)=\overline{\mathcal{F}u_{0}(\xi)}
.
So we can not deal with any non-trivial real-valued solutions under that condition.
This is the major and huge disadvantage for applications, and it is the same feature as in the previous papers
[
14
,
8
,
7
,
34
]
.
All the surprising generality rests on this price, which also precludes potential contradictions with the standard results.

For the symbols of the Fourier multipliers in (
1.1
), we assume

(C-s) Coefficients of semi-linear type:

\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
are smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}(\mathrm{v})
such that

\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}e^{t\hat{L}(\xi)}(t^{\theta}\hat{N}(\xi))^{j}
and
\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\hat{M}(\xi)

are bounded for
j\in\{0,1\}
,
0<t<1
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{(1/n,n)}(\mathrm{v})
,
for some
\theta\in[0,1)
, every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
.
Without the derivative (
\alpha=0
), they are required to be bounded also on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{(0,1)}(\mathrm{v})
.

Note that the symbols are allowed to be discontinuous at the boundary
\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi=0
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and assume
(C-s)
. Then the Cauchy problem of (
1.1
) is globally wellposed in
(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v}))^{n_{1}}
on
t\geq 0
.

See Corollary
4.2
and Theorem
4.1
for the more precise versions.

The above result is general but also restrictive about the equation when
d\geq 2
, since the symbols are required to be uniformly bounded in the orthogonal directions.
Roughly speaking, the nonlinear part of the equation is required to have less derivatives in the orthogonal directions than the linear smoothing effect of
L
.
We may remove this limitation by imposing a stronger condition on the Fourier support than the half space. For any closed subset
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{v}):=\{F\in\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})\mid\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathcal{R}\}.\end{split}

(1.5)

Since
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is continuous, it is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})
.
In this case, we may deal with much more general symbols, namely

(C-0) Coefficients bounded at
0
:

\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
are smooth on a neighborhood of
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}(\mathrm{v})
and bounded as
\xi\to 0
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, and
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}(\mathrm{v})
be a closed subset such that
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
and that
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v})
is bounded for each
a\in(0,\infty)
. Assume
(C-0)
. Then the Cauchy problem of (
1.1
) is globally wellposed in
(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{v}))^{n_{1}}
on
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

It is proven after Corollary
4.2
as an immediate consequence.
The simplest choice of
\mathcal{R}
is the cone
\{|\xi|\leq\lambda(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi)\}
with a constant
\lambda>0
, which approaches
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}(\mathrm{v})
as
\lambda\to\infty
.
The left side
|\xi|
may be replaced with any norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Another example is the octant
[0,\infty)^{d}
with
\mathrm{v}\in(0,\infty)^{d}
.
More generally,
\mathcal{R}
may grow arbitrarily fast as
\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi\to\infty
: For any
C^{1}
function
R:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
R(0)=0
, there exists
\mathcal{R}\supset\{|\xi|\leq R(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi)\}
with the require properties (cf. Lemma
4.4
).

In the above results, the symbols may grow arbitrarily fast as
|\xi|\to\infty
on
\mathcal{R}
(which is possible only as
\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi\to\infty
), meaning that the equation may contain arbitrary order of derivatives, including infinity.
Hence the global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{v})
holds even for extremely bad PDEs such as

\begin{split}u_{t}=-\Delta u+u^{3},\quad u_{t}=e^{e^{-\Delta}}[e^{(e^{-\Delta}u)^{2}}-1],\end{split}

(1.6)

where the sign in front of
\Delta
is the opposite one from the usual settings.
Such equations are not even locally wellposed in the standard function spaces, and the ODE versions are not globally wellposed either.
See Section
5.2
for another example, where the solutions may live outside of the natural boundary of the analytic nonlinearity
\mathscr{H}
.

The above generality indicates how strong is the assumption of the Fourier support, which essentially eliminates all possible nonlinear feedback.
In fact, the core of the proof is to construct weight functions in the Fourier space, depending on time, which control the nonlinear flow of frequencies to
\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi\to\infty
, so that the standard contraction argument can work on the subspaces forming
\mathcal{X}
characterized by the weights.
The main novelty compared with the previous work
[
14
,
8
,
7
,
34
]
is that the procedure works without using any explicit form of the equations or the weights.
This flexibility is crucial to get the wellposedness in the entire function space
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
.

1.2.
Solutions locally integrable in frequency

Because of the vast generality of the equation and the initial data, it is not easy to extract concrete information on the behavior of solutions and the weights from the proof in
\mathcal{X}
.
Restricting the initial data to the Fourier transform of locally integrable functions, we may simplify the construction of solutions considerably, with more explicit information on the behavior.
This version is still more general than the previous work on the equations and the initial data.

In this case, we first fix a closed
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}(\mathrm{v})
satisfying
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
, together with a continuous and super-additive function
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
(namely
\mathfrak{p}(\xi)+\mathfrak{p}(\eta)\leq\mathfrak{p}(\xi+\eta)
). Then for the Fourier multipliers in (
1.1
), we assume

(C-m) Measurable coefficients:

\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
are measurable on
\mathcal{R}
satisfying

\begin{split}\sigma_{\max}(\hat{L}+\hat{L}^{*})\leq 2\theta\mathfrak{p},\quad\max_{j,k}|\hat{N}_{j,k}|+|\hat{M}_{l,m}|\lesssim 1+\mathfrak{p},\end{split}

(1.7)

for all
\xi\in\mathcal{R}
and some constant
\theta\in(0,1)
, where
\hat{L}^{*}
is the adjoint of
\hat{L}
and

\sigma_{\max}
is the maximal eigenvalue of the self-adjoint matrix.

If
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfies (
1.7
) with locally bounded
|\xi|^{-1}\mathfrak{p}(\xi)
, then there exists
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
that is continuous and super-additive satisfying
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}\geq\mathfrak{p}
, by Lemma
4.4
. Then we may replace
\mathfrak{p}
with
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Next, for any
s<0
, we consider the weighted
L^{1}
space

\begin{split}&Z^{s}_{0}:=\{\varphi\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathcal{R},\ \|\varphi\|_{Z^{s}_{0}}:=\|(\mathfrak{p}^{-1}+\mathfrak{p})e^{s\mathfrak{p}}\varphi\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{R})}<\infty\}.\end{split}

(1.8)

More generally, we may choose any Banach space
Z^{s}\subset L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying

\begin{split}\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathcal{R},\quad\sup_{c\in\mathcal{R}}\|\varphi(\xi-c)\|_{Z^{s}}+\|\varphi\|_{Z^{s}_{0}}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{Z^{s}}=\||\varphi|\|_{Z^{s}}\end{split}

(1.9)

for all
\varphi\in Z^{s}
.
Z^{s}_{0}
is the maximal choice for
Z^{s}
. Another example is the anisotropic space for
\xi:=(\bar{\xi},\tilde{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-d_{0}}
with

\begin{split}&\mathfrak{p}(\xi):=\lambda(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi+(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi)^{m}),\quad\mathcal{R}:=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid|\bar{\xi}|\leq\lambda\mathfrak{p}(\xi)\},\\
&Z^{s}:=\{\varphi\in Z^{s}_{0}\mid\|\varphi\|_{Z^{s}}:=\|(\mathfrak{p}^{-\sigma}+1)\varphi\|_{L^{r}_{\bar{\xi}}L^{1}_{\tilde{\xi}}}<\infty\},\end{split}

(1.10)

for some
d_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,
\lambda>0
,
m\geq 1
,
r\in[1,\infty]
and
\sigma>1-d_{0}/r^{\prime}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}(\mathrm{v})
be closed satisfying
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
, and let
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
be continuous and super-additive.
Assume
(C-m)
. Let
s<0
and let
Z^{s}\subset L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a Banach space satisfying (
1.9
).
Then for any
B\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
b\in[1,\infty)
such that
for any
u_{0}\in(\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{n_{1}}
satisfying
\operatorname{supp}\mathcal{F}u_{0}\subset\mathcal{R}
and
\|e^{s\mathfrak{p}^{2}}\mathcal{F}u_{0}\|_{Z^{s}}\leq B
,
the Cauchy problem of (
1.1
) has a unique global solution
u
on
t\geq 0
satisfying

e^{b(s\mathfrak{p}^{2}-t\mathfrak{p})}\mathcal{F}u(t)\in Z^{s}(L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty))^{n_{1}}
.

See Theorem
7.1
for the more precise version, including the case where nonlinearity
\mathscr{H}
is decaying at
0
in higher order.
Note that the above is not a wellposedness result, because the solution is in a much rougher space than the initial data, due to the loss of regularity.
In the previous case of
\mathcal{X}
, the wellposedness holds thanks to its topological structure, namely the inductive limit, but huge loss of regularity is going on inside
\mathcal{X}
.

1.3.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, we give the precise definition and some basic properties of the space
\mathcal{X}
.
It is a locally convex topological vector space, defined as the inductive limit of an uncountable directed system of Banach spaces in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We give a local basis at
0\in\mathcal{X}
, as well as characterization of bounded subsets and compact subsets of
\mathcal{X}
. The latter is crucial for the proof of wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
.

In Section
3
, we investigate the convolution in those Banach spaces forming
\mathcal{X}
.
Lemma
3.4
is crucial for the contraction argument to solve the Cauchy problem, where the point is that arbitrarily small factors may be gained from the convolution on the half space with the boundary decay, if the weights are appropriately chosen.

In Section
4
, we prove the global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
, including the cases of stringer support conditions. Solutions periodic in space are also considered as the special case where the support is a lattice.

In Section
5
, we compare those solutions in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
with others, observing for the simplest ODE and for the Burgers equation that they go beyond the classical blow-up.
The example shows also that the global wellposedness can not hold within the tempered distributions.
Another example shows that the global solutions exist even if the initial data are completely outside of the natural boundary of the nonlinearity
\mathscr{H}
.

In Section
6
, we investigate optimality of the space
\mathcal{X}
or the conditions
(X-i)
–
(X-ii)
, as well as the stronger support condition, for the very general wellposedness to hold, using again the simple ODE.

In Section
7
, we consider the solutions locally integrable in the Fourier space, and give a much simpler argument for the unique global existence of solutions.

Finally in Section
8
, our results are applied to several examples of equations.

1.4.
Notations

The sets of non-negative integers are denoted by
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0,1,2,\ldots,\}
and
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\ldots,\}
.

1_{A}
denotes the characteristic function of any set
A
.

In any seminormed space
V
, open and closed balls are denoted by

\begin{split}B_{V}(c,r):=\{y\in V\mid\|c-y\|_{V}<r\},\quad\bar{B}_{V}(c,r):=\{y\in V\mid\|c-y\|_{V}\leq r\},\end{split}

(1.11)

with center
c\in V
and radius
r>0
.
For vectors
x\in V^{n}
of finite dimension
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the seminorm is defined by
\|x\|_{V}:=[\sum_{k=1,\ldots,n}\|x_{k}\|_{V}^{2}]^{1/2}
.
For two normed spaces
X,Y
, the Banach space of linear bounded operators from
X
to
Y
is denoted by
\mathcal{B}(X\to Y)
.
The normed space of
m\times n
complex matrices is denoted by
M(m,n)=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
That of square matrices is abbreviated
M(n)=M(n,n)
. The adjoint and the transpose of
A\in M(m,n)
are respectively denoted by
A^{*},A^{\intercal}\in M(n,m)
. For any vector
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the components are denoted by

\begin{split}\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})=(\xi_{1},\xi^{\perp})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}.\end{split}

(1.12)

The
L^{2}
inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by

\begin{split}{\langle f|g\rangle}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}dx.\end{split}

(1.13)

W^{m,p}(\Omega)
denotes the
L^{p}
-Sobolev space of regularity
m\in\mathbb{R}
on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the inhomogeneous
L^{p}
-Besov space of regularity
s\in\mathbb{R}
and interpolation index
q\in[1,\infty]
.

\mathscr{D}(\Omega)
is the space of smooth test functions compactly supported on
\Omega
, and
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the Schwartz class.
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
is the dual space or the space of distributions, and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of tempered distributions.
All the function spaces are considered with complex values.

For any
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(\mathrm{v})
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{(a,b)}(\mathrm{v})
by (
1.2
),
and similarly
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq a}(\mathrm{v})
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>a}(\mathrm{v})
, and
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq a}(\mathrm{v})
.
When
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}:=(1,0,\ldots,0)
, it is omitted as
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}(e_{1})
, etc.
Similarly, for any
Z\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we denote

\begin{split}&Z_{<a}:=\{f\in Z\mid\operatorname{supp}f\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<a}\},\quad Z_{(a,b)}:=\{f\in Z\mid\operatorname{supp}f\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{(a,b)}\}\end{split}

(1.14)

as well as for the other subscripts.
More generally, for any
O\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote

\begin{split}Z_{O}:=\{f\in Z\mid\operatorname{supp}f\subset O\}.\end{split}

(1.15)

When
O
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathscr{D}_{O}
is often identified with
\mathscr{D}(O)
.
Strictly speaking, they are homeomorphic by the restriction to
O
and the zero extension to the outside.

For cut-off arguments, we fix a
C^{\infty}
function
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
satisfying

\begin{split}t\leq 1\implies\chi(t)=1,\quad t\geq 2\implies\chi(t)=0.\end{split}

(1.16)

For any
l>0
, define
\chi^{l}(t):=\chi(t/l)
and
\chi_{l}(t):=1-\chi(t/l)
,
so that we have

\begin{split}&\operatorname{supp}\chi^{l}\subset(-\infty,2l),\quad\operatorname{supp}\chi_{l}\subset(l,\infty).\end{split}

(1.17)

The smooth cut-off operators in
\xi_{1}
are defined by

\begin{split}(X^{l}\varphi)(\xi):=\chi^{l}(\xi_{1})\varphi(\xi),\quad(X_{l}\varphi)(\xi):=\chi_{l}(\xi_{1})\varphi(\xi).\end{split}

(1.18)

For mollifiers, we fix
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying

\begin{split}0\leq\zeta(\xi)=\zeta(|\xi|),\quad\operatorname{supp}\zeta\subset B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(0,1),\quad\|\zeta\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1,\end{split}

(1.19)

and let
\zeta_{n}(\xi):=n^{d}\zeta(n\xi)\to\delta(\xi)
in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
.

For any
\delta\geq 0
and any interval
I\subset[0,\infty)
, let

\begin{split}&C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}:=\{f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)\mid\text{continuous and non-decreasing}\},\\
&C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}}}}:=\{f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)\mid\text{continuous and non-increasing}\},\\
&C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{I}:=\{f\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}\mid f([0,\infty))\subset I\},\quad C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}}}}_{I}:=\{f\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}}}}\mid f([0,\infty))\subset I\},\\
&C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{\delta}:=\{f\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}\mid f(0)=0,\ f([0,2\delta])\subset[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]\},\\
&C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{+}:=\{f\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}\mid f(0)=0,\ t>0\implies f(t)>0\}.\end{split}

(1.20)

C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{\delta}
is closed for
\max
, so it is a directed set for the pointwise order.
Note that
C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}=\bigcup_{0<\delta\leq 1}C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{\delta}\supset C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{+}
. We will use
C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
as an index set for the inductive limit to construct function spaces of distributions.

For topological vector spaces
X,Y
, the continuous embedding is denoted by
X\hookrightarrow Y
.
For any
m\in[0,\infty)
, the Banach space of bounded
C^{m}
functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{m}:=\{\varphi\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\mid\|\varphi\|_{m}:=\max_{|\alpha|\leq m}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi(\xi)|<\infty\}.\end{split}

(1.21)

Note that
\mathcal{B}^{m}=\mathcal{B}^{m_{0}}
for the largest
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
m_{0}\leq m
.
The trivial extension to non-integer
m
is for notational convenience.
Let
\mathcal{B}^{0}_{0}
be the closure of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
\mathcal{B}^{0}
and let
\mathcal{M}:=(\mathcal{B}^{0}_{0})^{\prime}
be its dual space.
Each
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}
may be regarded as a (bounded) complex-valued Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, whose total variation is a finite Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denoted by
|\mathfrak{m}|
, satisfying

\begin{split}|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d})/2\leq\|\mathfrak{m}\|_{(\mathcal{B}^{0}_{0})^{\prime}}=:\|\mathfrak{m}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq 2|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}).\end{split}

(1.22)

We also introduce the subspace vanishing on the left:

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{+}:=\{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}\mid|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq 0})=0\},\end{split}

(1.23)

then the Dirichlet condition
(X-i)
with
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
is equivalent to existence of
l>0
such that
X^{l}F\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
.
See also Definitions
2.1
–
2.2
for some other function spaces.

We define the Fourier transform for
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\begin{split}\hat{\varphi}(\xi)=\mathcal{F}\varphi(\xi):=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)e^{-ix\xi}dx,\quad\mathcal{F}^{-1}\psi(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(\xi)e^{i\xi x}d\xi\end{split}

(1.24)

which is extended as usual to the tempered distributions
F\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by duality

\begin{split}(\mathcal{F}F)(\varphi)=\hat{F}(\varphi):=F(\hat{\varphi}),\end{split}

(1.25)

for
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The above definition of
\mathcal{F}
may be less conventional, but it simplifies the constant in the convolution, namely, for any
f\in\mathscr{S}
and any
g\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(fg)=(\mathcal{F}f)*(\mathcal{F}g).\end{split}

(1.26)

The Fourier inverse image of the entire space of distributions

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as the dual space of

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\{\mathcal{F}\varphi\mid\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{split}

(1.27)

which has the natural topology induced by
\mathcal{F}
from
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that
\mathcal{F}:\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is homeomorphic.
These spaces have been studied from the early time of distributions;

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the same as
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
in
[
13
]
and
Z^{\prime}
in
[
16
, Chapter II]
.

The Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by (
1.25
) for
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, satisfying the same algebraic formulas as in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
.
In particular, (
1.26
) holds for
f\in\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}
and
g\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
.
It is immediate from the definition that
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is homeomorphic, which justifies the notation
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
More generally, for any topological linear space
X\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, its Fourier inverse image is denoted by

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{-1}X:=\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}f\mid f\in X\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{split}

(1.28)

with the natural topology induced by
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
, or the initial topology for
\mathcal{F}
, so that
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{F}^{-1}X\to X
is homeomorphic.

Since
\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}
is dense, so is
\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}
, hence we have

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is also dense.
Indeed, for the standard Littlewood-Paley decomposition using a dyadic partition in the Fourier space
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, each piece
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni F\mapsto\varphi_{j}*F\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is continuous, and
F=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{j}*F
on
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
In such a way,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a natural space for the analysis of PDE on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, in contrast with
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In the latter, the Littlewood-Paley decomposition is not well-defined,
and both the heat and Schrödinger equations are illposed in both time directions, whereas they are wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
in both time directions.

Note that if
\mathcal{F}
is replaced with
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
in the above definition, then we have

\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}
including the topology, so we could naturally write
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
, but we refrain from using this identification, to avoid possible ambiguity or confusion.
It is also worth noting that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{F}\mathcal{X}=\{0\}
because of the anisotropy.

2.
Spaces of distributions for the Fourier transform of solutions

Here we introduce some function spaces consisting of distributions in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for the Fourier transform of the solutions to the Cauchy problem (
1.1
).
In the following, we fix
\mathrm{v}:=e_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
, without losing generality, since the other case follows just by a linear transform of the spatial coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The main purpose of this section is to define and characterize the function space
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
. For
\delta,m\in[0,\infty)
, we define

\begin{split}&\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}:=\max_{|\alpha|\leq m}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}}|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi(\xi)|,\quad\|\varphi\|_{m,\delta}:=\|X^{\delta}\varphi\|_{0}^{+}+\|X_{\delta}\varphi\|_{m}^{+},\\
&\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+}:=\{\varphi\in C^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\mid\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}<\infty\},\quad\mathcal{B}^{\infty}_{+}:=\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+}.\end{split}

(2.1)

For any
l,m\in[0,\infty)
, and
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define

\begin{split}\rho_{l}^{m}(F):=\sup\{\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}F(\varphi)\mid\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l},\ \|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}\leq 1\}.\end{split}

(2.2)

The functional
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
may be extended to
\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+<l}
if
\rho^{m}_{l}(F)<\infty
, using that
\mathscr{D}_{<l}\subset C^{m}_{<l}
is dense for the
C^{m}
topology (namely for the local uniform convergence including the partial derivatives up to order
m
).
This extension is needed to define the convolution.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
l>0
,
m\geq 0
and
\rho_{l}^{m}(F)<\infty
.
Then
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0}
and

\begin{split}\lim_{R\to\infty}\rho_{l}^{m}(\chi_{R}(|\xi|)F)=0.\end{split}

(2.3)

Hence
F
is extended to a unique linear functional
\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+<l}\to\mathbb{C}
that is continuous with respect to the
C^{m}
topology.
Moreover, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+<l}
,

\begin{split}|F(\varphi)|\leq\rho_{l}^{m}(F)\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}.\end{split}

(2.4)

Note that the space
\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+}
does not assume any decay or uniform continuity for
|\xi|\to\infty
.

Proof.

For
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
, (
2.4
) is immediate from the definition.
If
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<0}
, then
\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}=0
implies
F(\varphi)=0
, hence
\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
.
Next we prove (
2.3
).
If it is false, then there exist sequences
\varphi_{n}\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
and
R_{n}\to\infty
such that
\|\varphi_{n}\|_{m}^{+}\leq 1
,
F(\chi_{R_{n}}(|\xi|)\varphi_{n})=1
, and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{n}\subset B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(0,R_{n+1})
. Let

\begin{split}\psi_{N}:=\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\chi_{R_{n}}(|\xi|)\varphi_{n}\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}.\end{split}

(2.5)

Since the summand is supported on
R_{n}<|\xi|<R_{n+1}
, which is mutually disjoint, we have
\|\psi_{N}\|_{m}^{+}\lesssim 1
, whereas
F(\psi_{N})=N
, contradicting (
2.4
) on
\mathscr{D}_{<l}
.
Hence (
2.3
) is true.
It implies that
F|_{\mathscr{D}_{<l}}
is continuous with respect to the
C^{m}
topology.
Then its unique extension to
\mathcal{B}^{m}_{+<l}
is given by the density of
\mathscr{D}_{<l}
.
In fact, it may be defined by using mollifiers,

F(\varphi):=\lim_{n\to\infty}F(\zeta_{n}*\chi^{n}(|\xi|)\varphi)
.
∎

Definition 2.2
.

For
\mu,\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
,
l\in(0,\infty)
, and
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, define

\begin{split}&\rho^{\mu,\nu}_{l}(F):=\inf\{B\geq 0\mid 0<t\leq l\implies\rho^{\mu(t)}_{t}(F)\leq B\nu(t)\},\quad(\inf\varnothing=\infty),\\
&\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F):=\sup_{l>0}\rho^{\mu,\nu}_{l}(F),\quad\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}:=\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F)<\infty\}.\end{split}

(2.6)

\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Banach space.
For any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\nu_{0},\nu_{1}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
satisfying
(\mu_{0},\nu_{0})\leq(\mu_{1},\nu_{1})
(pointwisely), we have

\rho^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}(F)\leq\rho^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0}}(F)
and
\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}
, so that we may consider their union as the inductive limit in the category of locally convex topological vector spaces, denoted by

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}:=\bigcup_{\mu,\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{668184}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{668184}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{598024}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{686239}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4861pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}}\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).\end{split}

(2.7)

The subscript
0
in the index set
C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
requires
\mu(0)=\nu(0)=0
, which is corresponding to the boundary condition
(X-i)
at
\xi_{1}=0
, and crucial for our analysis of the PDE (
1.1
).
When the direction
e_{1}
is replaced with a general
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, it is defined by

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v}):=\{F(U_{\mathrm{v}}\xi)\mid F\in\mathcal{X}\},\end{split}

(2.8)

where
U_{\mathrm{v}}
is any invertible linear transform of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
U_{\mathrm{v}}\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
.

In this paper, we consider the inductive limits only in the category of locally convex topological vector spaces. See e.g.,
[
6
]
about basic properties of them, though we will use only a few of them.

\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
ensures that
\mathcal{X}
is Hausdorff.
Roughly speaking,
-\mu(l),\nu(l)
are respectively the regularity order and the size of
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
.
By definition, we have for any
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
,
l>0
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
,

\begin{split}|F(\varphi)|\leq\nu(l)\rho^{\mu,\nu}_{l}(F)\|\varphi\|_{\mu(l)}^{+},\end{split}

(2.9)

where
\rho^{\mu,\nu}_{l}(F)
is the optimal non-negative number satisfying this inequality. Lemma
2.1
extends it to
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{\mu(l)}_{+<l}
.
If
\mu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
then
\mu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{\delta}
for some
\delta>0
,
\mu(l)\leq 1/2<1
for
l\leq 2\delta
, so that we may weaken the norm for
\varphi
as

\begin{split}|F(\varphi)|&\leq|F(X^{\delta}\varphi)|+|F(X_{\delta}\varphi)|\leq\nu(l)\rho^{\mu,\nu}_{l}(F)\|\varphi\|_{\mu(l),\delta}.\end{split}

(2.10)

To change the size weight
\nu
, we have

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
\kappa\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
,
\mu,\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
,
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
l\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\begin{split}\rho^{\mu,\nu}_{l}(F)\leq\kappa(l)\rho^{\mu,\kappa\nu}_{l}(F).\end{split}

(2.11)

Let
\mu_{j},\nu_{j}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
(
j=0,1
) and
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0}})
satisfy

\begin{split}\rho^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0}}_{l}(TF)\leq B(l)\rho^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}_{l}(F),\end{split}

(2.12)

for some non-deacreasing
B:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, all
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}
and all
l\in(0,\infty)
. Then for any bounded
\kappa\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
, any
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{1},\kappa\nu_{1}}
and
l\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\begin{split}\rho^{\mu_{0},\kappa\nu_{0}}_{l}(TF)\leq B(l)\rho^{\mu_{1},\kappa\nu_{1}}_{l}(F).\end{split}

(2.13)

Proof.

For any
0<t\leq l<\infty
and
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
, we have

\begin{split}\rho^{\mu(t)}_{t}(F)\leq\rho^{\mu,\kappa\nu}_{l}(F)\kappa(t)\nu(t)\leq\kappa(l)\rho^{\mu,\kappa\nu}_{l}(F)\nu(t),\end{split}

(2.14)

which implies (
2.11
).
Using (
2.11
), we have

\begin{split}\rho^{\mu_{0}(t)}_{t}(TF)&\leq\nu_{0}(t)\rho^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0}}_{t}(TF)\leq\nu_{0}(t)B(t)\rho^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}_{t}(F)\leq\nu_{0}(t)B(l)\kappa(t)\rho^{\mu_{1},\kappa\nu_{1}}_{t}(F),\end{split}

(2.15)

which implies (
2.13
).
The boundedness of
\kappa
was used merely to ensure
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}
.
∎

In the analysis of the space
\mathcal{X}
, a basic tool is the smooth cut-off
X_{\delta},X^{\delta}
in
\xi_{1}
.
Here we prepare a few estimates on them.
For the factor coming from the Leibniz rule, we define
M_{\delta}^{\chi}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{[1,\infty)}
for
all
\delta\in(0,1]
, first on
[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]\cup\mathbb{N}
by

\begin{split}M_{\delta}^{\chi}(l)=\begin{cases}1+l,&(0\leq l\leq\tfrac{1}{2}),\\
\sum_{k=0}^{l}\tfrac{l!}{k!(l-k)!}\|\partial^{k}\chi_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})},&(l\in\mathbb{N}),\end{cases}\end{split}

(2.16)

and then extend it to
[0,\infty)
as a continuous and increasing function, e.g., the piecewise linear function.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\delta,l\in(0,\infty)
,
m\in[0,\infty)
and
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\begin{split}\rho^{m}_{l}(X_{\delta}F)\leq M^{\chi}_{\delta}(m)\rho^{m}_{l}(F),\quad\rho^{m}_{l}(X^{\delta}F)\leq M^{\chi}_{\delta}(m)\rho^{m}_{\min(l,2\delta)}(F).\end{split}

(2.17)

Let
\mu,\nu,\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
satisfying
\mu\leq\tilde{\mu}
. Let
0<L,H,l<\infty
and
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(1)

If
M^{\chi}_{L}(\mu)\nu\leq\tilde{\nu}
on
[L,\infty)
,
then
\rho_{l}^{\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}}(X_{L}F)\leq\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F)
.

(2)

If
M^{\chi}_{H}(\mu)\nu\leq\tilde{\nu}
on
(0,2H]
,
then
\rho_{l}^{\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}}(X^{H}F)\leq\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F)
.

(3)

If
M^{\chi}_{L}(\mu)M^{\chi}_{H}(\mu)\nu\leq\tilde{\nu}
on
[L,2H]
, then
\rho_{l}^{\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}}(X_{L}X^{H}F)\leq\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F)
.

Proof.

By the Leibniz rule, we have for any
m\in[0,\infty)
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}
,

\begin{split}\max(\|X^{\delta}\varphi\|_{m}^{+},\|X_{\delta}\varphi\|_{m}^{+})&\leq\sup_{|\alpha|\leq m}\sum_{0\leq k\leq\alpha_{1}}\tfrac{\alpha_{1}!}{(\alpha_{1}-k)!k!}\|\chi_{\delta}^{(k)}(\xi_{1})\|_{0}^{+}\|\partial^{\alpha-ke_{1}}\varphi\|_{0}^{+}\\
&\leq M^{\chi}_{\delta}(m)\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}.\end{split}

(2.18)

Hence for any
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
we have, using
\operatorname{supp}X^{\delta}\varphi\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<2\delta}
as well,

\begin{split}&|X_{\delta}F(\varphi)|\leq\rho^{m}_{l}(F)\|X_{\delta}\varphi\|_{m}^{+}\leq\rho^{m}_{l}(F)M^{\chi}_{\delta}(m)\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+},\\
&|X^{\delta}F(\varphi)|\leq\rho^{m}_{\min(l,2\delta)}(F)\|X^{\delta}\varphi\|_{m}^{+}\leq\rho^{m}_{\min(l,2\delta)}(F)M^{\chi}_{\delta}(m)\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}.\end{split}

(2.19)

from which the estimates in
\rho^{m}_{l}
follow.

In the case of (1), we have
X_{L}F(\varphi)=0
for
l\leq L
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
, while for
l\geq L
,

\begin{split}\rho^{\tilde{\mu}(l)}_{l}(X_{L}F)\leq\rho^{\mu(l)}_{l}(X_{L}F)\leq M^{\chi}_{L}(\mu(l))\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F)\nu(l)\leq\tilde{\nu}(l)\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F).\end{split}

(2.20)

In the case of (2), we have similarly for all
l>0
and
l^{\prime}:=\min(l,2H)

\begin{split}\rho^{\tilde{\mu}(l)}_{l}(X^{H}F)\leq\rho^{\mu(l^{\prime})}_{l^{\prime}}(X^{H}F)&\leq M^{\chi}_{H}(\mu(l^{\prime}))\rho^{\mu(l^{\prime})}_{l^{\prime}}(F)\leq\tilde{\nu}(l)\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F).\end{split}

(2.21)

In the case of (3), we have similarly for
l\geq L
,

\begin{split}\rho^{\tilde{\mu}(l)}_{l}(X_{L}X^{H}F)\leq M^{\chi}_{L}(\mu(l^{\prime}))M^{\chi}_{H}(\mu(l^{\prime}))\rho^{\mu(l^{\prime})}_{l^{\prime}}(F)\leq\tilde{\nu}(l)\rho_{l}^{\mu,\nu}(F).\end{split}

(2.22)

Thus we obtain the estimates in
\mathcal{X}^{\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}}
for all the cases (1)–(3).
∎

2.1.
Characterization of
\mathcal{X}

Now we see that the definition of
\mathcal{X}
in Section
1.1
is the same as in this section.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
F\in\mathcal{X}
is equivalent to
(X-i)
–
(X-ii)
with
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
.
Moreover, for any
F\in\mathcal{X}
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\begin{split}F(\varphi)=\lim_{l\to+0}F(X_{l}\varphi).\end{split}

(2.23)

The above identity (
2.23
) allows us to ignore the test functions
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq 0}
. More precisely, let
\mathscr{R}:\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0})
denote the restriction from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
,
and for any
G\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0})
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, let

\begin{split}\mathscr{E}G(\varphi):=\lim_{l\to+0}G(X_{l}\varphi).\end{split}

(2.24)

The limit does not converge for general
G\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0})
, but when it does for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
\mathscr{E}G\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. (
2.23
) may be rewritten as:
F=\mathscr{E}\mathscr{R}F
for all
F\in\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

For any
\delta>0
,
\mu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{\delta}
,
\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and any
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
,
Lemma
2.1
implies
\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}

and (
2.9
) for any
l>0
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{\mu(l)}_{+<l}
.
In particular,

\begin{split}|(X^{\delta}F)(\varphi)|\leq\nu(l)\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F)\|\varphi\|_{0},\end{split}

(2.25)

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}^{0}
, so we have
X^{\delta}F\in\mathcal{M}
with the total variation satisfying

\begin{split}|X^{\delta}F|(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l})\leq\nu(l)\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F)\to 0\quad(l\to+0).\end{split}

(2.26)

Hence by the dominated convergence,
X^{\delta}F\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
,
It also implies
F(X^{l}\varphi)\to 0
as
l\to+0
for any
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}
, hence
F(\varphi)=\mathscr{E}\mathscr{R}F(\varphi)
.

For larger
\xi_{1}
, it suffices to show for each
l>0
that
X^{l}F\in W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
m-1\geq\mu(2l)
.
Henceforth we ignore the dependence of constants on
F,l,m
.
Define
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{|\alpha|\leq m}\subset\mathbb{Z}
by

\begin{split}(1-\Delta)^{m}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}c_{\alpha}\partial^{2\alpha},\end{split}

(2.27)

and let
\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)}:=(1-\Delta)^{-m}c_{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha}\zeta_{n}\in\mathscr{S}
and
\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)\dagger}(\xi):=\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)}(-\xi)
. Then
we have
\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}\partial^{\alpha}\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)}=\zeta_{n}
.
Let
f_{n}:=\zeta_{n}*X^{l}F
. Then
f_{n}\in C^{\infty}
, and we have from (
2.9
),

\begin{split}|{\langle f_{n}|\bar{\varphi}\rangle}|=|X^{l}F(\zeta_{n}*\varphi)|\lesssim\|\zeta_{n}*\varphi\|_{m-1}\lesssim n^{m-1}\|\varphi\|_{0}\end{split}

(2.28)

for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}
, which implies
\|f_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim n^{m-1}
. Similarly we have, for
|\beta|\leq 1
,

\begin{split}|{\langle\partial^{\beta}\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)}*f_{n}|\bar{\varphi}\rangle}|&=|X^{l}F(\partial^{\beta}\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)\dagger}*\zeta_{n}*\varphi)|\\
&\lesssim\|\partial^{\beta}\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)\dagger}*\zeta_{n}*\varphi\|_{m-1}\lesssim\|\zeta_{n}*\varphi\|_{0}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{0}.\end{split}

(2.29)

Hence
\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)}*f_{n}
is bounded in
W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and so convergent along a subsequence in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
|\alpha|\leq m
.
Let
g_{\alpha}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the limit. Then

\begin{split}X^{l}F(\varphi)=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\langle\zeta_{n}*f_{n}|\bar{\varphi}\rangle}&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}{\langle\psi_{n}^{(\alpha)}*f_{n}|(-\partial)^{\alpha}\bar{\varphi}\rangle}\\
&=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}{\langle g_{\alpha}|(-\partial)^{\alpha}\bar{\varphi}\rangle}.\end{split}

(2.30)

Hence
X^{l}F=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}\partial^{\alpha}g_{\alpha}\in W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thus
(X-i)
–
(X-ii)
are satisfied.

Conversely, suppose that
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies
(X-i)
–
(X-ii)
.
By
(X-i)
, we have
|F(\varphi)|\leq 2|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l})\|\varphi\|_{0}^{+}
for
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
and small
l>0
,
and by
(X-ii)
,
|F(\varphi)|\leq\|F|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}}\|_{W^{-m,1}}\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}
for any
l>0
,
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{(0,l)}
and some
m=m(l)\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l})\to|\mathfrak{m}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\leq 0})=0
as
l\to+0
,
we may find
\mu,\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
such that (
2.9
) holds for all
l>0
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<l}
.
Then
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}\subset\mathcal{X}
.
∎

2.2.
Local basis in
\mathcal{X}

Here we characterize the topology of
\mathcal{X}
by constructing a local basis at
0
.
For any
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\nu\in C_{+}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
, let

\begin{split}\mathscr{B}^{m}(\nu):=\{F\in\mathcal{X}\mid\sup_{l>0}\rho^{m}_{l}(F)/\nu(l)\leq 1\}.\end{split}

(2.31)

For any
R_{0},R_{1}\in(0,\infty)
and any
N=\{N_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{+}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
, let

\begin{split}&\mathscr{B}(N):=\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}\mathscr{B}^{m}(N_{m}),\\
&\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N):=\bar{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{+}}(0,R_{0})+\mathcal{X}_{\geq R_{1}}+\mathscr{B}(N).\end{split}

(2.32)

Since
\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)
is the increasing limit of the sum of balls and a subspace, it is an absolutely convex subset of
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
be the collection of all such
\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)
.

Proposition 2.5
.

\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is a local basis at
0
of
\mathcal{X}
.
In other words, a subset of
\mathcal{X}
is a neighborhood of
0\in\mathcal{X}
iff it contains a member of
\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

First we show that every
\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)\in\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is a
0
-neighborhood.
Since it is absolutely convex, by definition of the inductive limit
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{\mu,\nu}\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
,
it is equivalent that its intersection with
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
is a
0
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
for every
\mu,\nu\in C_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
.

Let
\delta\in(0,R_{1}/4)
,
\mu\in C_{\delta}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
,
\nu\in C_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
and
F\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
.
Let
F_{0}:=X^{\delta}F
,
F_{2}:=X_{R_{1}}F
and
F_{1}:=X_{\delta}X^{R_{1}}F
. Then

F_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
\|F_{0}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\nu(2\delta)\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F)
,
F_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{(\delta,2R_{1})}
and
F_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{\geq R_{1}}
.
By Lemma
2.3
,
X_{\delta}X^{R_{1}}
is bounded
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}\to\mathcal{X}^{m,N_{m}}
for
m:=\mu(2R_{1})
. Hence if
\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F)
is small enough
then
\|F_{0}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq R_{0}
,
\rho^{m,N_{m}}(F_{1})\leq 1
, and
F\in\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)
. So
\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
consists of
0
-neighborhoods in
\mathcal{X}
.

Next let
U\subset\mathcal{X}
be an absolutely convex
0
-neighborhood.
If
\bar{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{+}}(0,R_{0})\not\subset U
for all
R_{0}>0
, then there is a sequence
F_{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\setminus U
such that
\|F_{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}<1/n^{2}
. Let
\nu_{n}(l):=\|X^{2l}F_{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}

and
\nu:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}n\nu_{n}
.
Then
n\nu_{n}\leq n\|F_{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}<1/n
implies that
\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
, while

\rho^{0}_{l}(F_{n})\leq\nu_{n}(l)\leq\tfrac{1}{n}\nu(l)
for all
l>0

implies
\rho^{0,\nu}(F_{n})\leq\tfrac{1}{n}\to 0
, so
F_{n}\to 0
in
\mathcal{X}^{0,\nu}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
, contradicting
F_{n}\not\in U
. Hence
\bar{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{+}}(0,R_{0})\subset U
for some
R_{0}>0
.

Next, if there is no
R_{1}<\infty
such that
\mathcal{X}_{\geq R_{1}}\subset U
, then there are sequences
\mu_{n},\nu_{n}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and
F_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{n},\nu_{n}}_{>n}\setminus U
.
Define
\mu,\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
first at each
l\in\mathbb{N}
by

\begin{split}\mu(l)=\max_{1\leq n\leq l}\mu_{n}(l+1),\quad\nu(l)=\max_{1\leq n\leq l}n\rho^{\mu_{n},\nu_{n}}(F_{n})\nu_{n}(l+1),\end{split}

(2.33)

and then extend them to
[0,\infty)
.
Then we have
\mu_{n}(l)\leq\mu(l)
and
n\rho^{\mu_{n},\nu_{n}}(F_{n})\nu_{n}(l)\leq\nu(l)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
l\geq n
, so
\rho^{\mu(l)}_{l}(F_{n})\leq\nu_{n}(l)\rho^{\mu_{n},\nu_{n}}(F_{n})\leq\tfrac{1}{n}\nu(l)
, while

\operatorname{supp}F_{n}
implies
\rho^{\mu(l)}_{l}(F_{n})=0
for
l\leq n
.
Hence
\rho^{\mu,\nu}(F_{n})\leq\tfrac{1}{n}\to 0
, so
F_{n}\to 0
in
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
, contradicting
F_{n}\not\in U
. Hence
\mathcal{X}_{\geq R_{1}}\subset U
for some
R_{1}\in[1,\infty)
.

It remains to consider the
\mathscr{B}^{m}(N_{m})
part.
Let
R_{j}:=2^{-j+1}R_{1}
and
\nu_{j}(l):=\tfrac{l}{2R_{j}}\in C_{+}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
U\subset\mathcal{X}
is a
0
-neighborhood, there is
\{\varepsilon_{m,j}\}_{m,j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
such that

\bar{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{m\nu_{j},\nu_{j}}}(0,\varepsilon_{m,j})\subset U
.
Take any
\{N_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{+}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
satisfying for all
m,j\in\mathbb{N}

\begin{split}N_{m}(2R_{j})\leq[M^{\chi}_{R_{j+1}}(m)M^{\chi}_{R_{j}}(m)]^{-1}2^{-j-3}\varepsilon_{m,j}.\end{split}

(2.34)

For any
m,j\in\mathbb{N}
and
F\in\mathscr{B}^{m}(N_{m})
, let
F_{j}:=X_{R_{j+1}}X^{R_{j}}F
. Then Lemma
2.3
implies

\begin{split}\rho^{m\nu_{j},\nu_{j}}(F_{j})\leq\nu_{j}(R_{j+1})^{-1}2^{-j-3}\varepsilon_{m,j}\rho^{m,N_{m}}(F)\leq 2^{-j-1}\varepsilon_{m,j},\end{split}

(2.35)

so
F_{j}\in 2^{-j-1}U
, while
F\in\mathcal{X}
implies
\|X^{R_{J}}F\|_{\mathcal{M}}<R_{0}/4
for some
J\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\begin{split}F=X^{R_{J}}F+X_{R_{1}}F+\sum_{1\leq j<J}F_{j}\in U/4+U/4+\sum_{1\leq j<J}2^{-j-1}U\subset U,\end{split}

(2.36)

by the absolute convexity of
U
, and similarly

\mathscr{B}(R_{0}/4,R_{1},\{2^{-m-1}N_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}})\subset U
.
Hence
\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is a local basis of
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

The local basis
\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
of
\mathcal{X}
implies the following embeddings. For
\delta>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, define the spaces of distributions

\begin{split}&\mathcal{X}_{L}:=\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0}\mid\{X^{l}F\}_{l>0}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\},\\
&\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{M}}:=\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0}\mid\{X^{l}F\}_{l>0}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}\},\\
&W^{-m,1}_{\delta}:=\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq\delta}\mid\{X^{l}F\}_{l>0}\subset W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\},\end{split}

(2.37)

equipped with the initial topology for the family of mappings
\{X^{l}\}_{l>0}
. Then

\begin{split}L^{1}_{\geq 0}\hookrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{M}_{+}\\
&\mathcal{X}_{L}\end{aligned}\right\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{M}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X},\quad W^{-m,1}_{\delta}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}.\end{split}

(2.38)

Indeed,
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{M}
is a closed subspace with the same norm.
If
F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies
\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, then
F\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
follows from the dominated convergence, hence
L^{1}_{\geq 0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\mathcal{X}_{L}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

L^{1}_{\geq 0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{M}}
are obvious.

\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{M}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
because
\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)\supset(X^{R_{1}})^{-1}B_{\mathcal{M}_{+}}(0,R_{0})
, and

W^{-m,1}_{\delta}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
because

\begin{split}\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)\supset\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq\delta}\mid\|X^{R_{1}}F\|_{W^{-m,1}}<N_{m}(\delta)\},\end{split}

(2.39)

which follows from the duality
|G(\varphi)|\leq\|G\|_{W^{-m,1}}\|\varphi\|_{m}^{+}
for
G\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq\delta}
and
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}
.

To justify very weak solutions in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
, the density of nice functions is crucial, for which the flexibility of weights
\mu,\nu
is necessary. In fact,
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is not dense in any
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
, but we may construct approximate identities
I_{n}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathscr{D}_{>0}
as follows.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
\{c_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\{r_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
satisfy
|c_{n}|+r_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and
c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>r_{n}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\varphi_{n}(\xi):=r_{n}^{-d}\zeta(r_{n}^{-1}(\xi-c_{n}))
,
\psi_{n}(\xi):=\chi(r_{n}|\xi|)
and
I_{n}F:=\varphi_{n}*(\psi_{n}F)
. Then
I_{n}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathscr{D}_{>0}
is continuous for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
I_{n}F\to F
in
\mathcal{X}
as
n\to\infty
for all
F\in\mathcal{X}
.
For any
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
such that
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
and the closure of the interior contains
0\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we may choose
c_{n},r_{n}
such that
I_{n}
preserves
\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathcal{R}
.

Those
I_{n}
are just the standard mollification moving the support to the right.

Proof.

By definition,
\varphi_{n},\psi_{n}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{n}\subset B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(c_{n},r_{n})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
. Hence
F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0}
implies
I_{n}F\in\mathscr{D}_{>0}
.
If the closure of the interior of
\mathcal{R}
contains
0
, then we may choose
c_{n},r_{n}
such that
B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(c_{n},r_{n})\subset\mathcal{R}
. Then for
\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathcal{R}
, we have
\operatorname{supp}(I_{n}F)\subset\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{n}+\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
.
The continuity of
I_{n}
follows from that of
I_{n}:\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is well-known.

It remains to prove
I_{n}F\to F
in
\mathcal{X}
.
Take any
F\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathscr{B}(R_{0},R_{1},N)\in\mathscr{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
By Lemma
2.4
, there exist
\delta\in(0,R_{1}/2)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\begin{split}X^{\delta}F\in\mathcal{M}_{+},\quad\|X^{\delta}F\|_{\mathcal{M}}<\tfrac{1}{8}R_{0},\quad X^{R_{1}}F\in W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).\end{split}

(2.40)

Let
F=F^{0}+F^{1}+F^{2}
with
F^{0}:=X^{\delta}F
and
F^{2}:=X_{R_{1}}F
. Then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\begin{split}I_{n}F^{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{+},\quad\|I_{n}F^{0}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\|F^{0}\|_{\mathcal{M}}<\tfrac{1}{8}R_{0},\quad\operatorname{supp}I_{n}F^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>R_{1}},\end{split}

(2.41)

which implies
I_{n}(F^{0}+F^{2})-(F^{0}+F^{2})\in\tfrac{1}{8}(U+U+U+U)\subset\tfrac{1}{2}U
.
On the other hand,
F^{1}\in W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\operatorname{supp}F^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq\delta}

imply that
\operatorname{supp}I_{n}F^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq\delta}
and
I_{n}F^{1}\to F^{1}
in
W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
hence
I_{n}F^{1}\to F^{1}
in
W^{-m,1}_{\delta}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
by (
2.38
).
In particular
I_{n}F^{1}-F^{1}\in\tfrac{1}{2}U
for large
n
, then
I_{n}F-F\in U
.
Hence
I_{n}F\to F
in
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

2.3.
Bounded sets in
\mathcal{X}

Here we characterize the bounded subsets of
\mathcal{X}
.
First recall that the inductive limit
X=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
of a sequence of normed spaces
X_{1}\hookrightarrow X_{2}\hookrightarrow\cdots
is said to be
regular
if every bounded set
B\subset X
is a bounded set of some
X_{n}
. It is not always true, but
[
15
]
gives some sufficient conditions.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the negative Sobolev spaces of measure-type by

\begin{split}&\mathcal{W}^{m}:=\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\|F\|_{\mathcal{W}^{m}}<\infty\},\quad\mathcal{W}^{\infty}:=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mathcal{W}^{m}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
&\|F\|_{\mathcal{W}^{m}}:=\sup\{\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}F(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \|\varphi\|_{m}\leq 1\}.\end{split}

(2.42)

Then
\mathcal{W}^{m}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Banach space, whose closed unit ball
\bar{B}_{\mathcal{W}^{m}}(0,1)
is closed in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
because of the definition by test functions, and
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is reflexive. Hence
[
15
, Corollary 1]
implies that the inductive limit
\mathcal{W}^{\infty}
is regular.

Proposition 2.7
.

For any subset
B\subset\mathcal{X}
, its boundedness is equivalent to existence of
\mu\in C_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
and
\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
such that
\rho^{\mu(l)}_{l}(F)\leq\nu(l)
for all
F\in B
and
l>0
.

Remark 2.1
.

If
\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
(namely
\nu(0)=0
) then
B
is bounded in
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
, but we may not choose
\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
in general, which is easily observed already in the subspace
L^{1}_{\geq 0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
: For any non-zero
f\in\mathscr{D}_{>0}
,
the concentrating sequence of mass
\{nf(n\xi_{1},\xi^{\perp})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

is bounded in
L^{1}_{\geq 0}
, but in none of
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
. It is convergent in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0}
to a measure on the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
, which is not in
\mathcal{X}
.
In short,
\mathcal{X}=\bigcup_{\mu,\nu}\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu}
fails to be regular because of the decay condition
(X-i)
at
\xi_{1}=0
.

Proof.

Let
B\subset\mathcal{X}
be bounded. For every
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
X^{l}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{W}^{\infty}
is continuous, and
\mathcal{W}^{\infty}
is the regular inductive limit, so there exist some
