c,\tilde{L},\tilde{M}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{[1,\infty)}
satisfying (
4.7
) for all
t,l>0
,
\varphi\in(\mathscr{D}_{<l})^{n_{1}}
and
\psi\in(\mathscr{D}_{<l})^{n_{2}}
.
Then for any
\nu_{0}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and any
b_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
\nu\in C([0,\infty);C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0})
with
\nu(t)\geq\nu_{0}
for all
t\geq 0
such that for any
\hat{u}_{0}\in(\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{n_{1}}
satisfying
\rho^{\mu,\nu_{0}}(\hat{u}_{0})\leq b_{0}
, there is a unique global solution
u\in C([0,\infty);(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X})^{n_{1}})
of (
4.4
) satisfying
\rho^{\mu,\nu(t)}(\hat{u}(t))\in L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty)
.

Note that the growth condition (
4.7
) on the coefficients
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
depends on
\mu
or the growth of initial condition in
\xi_{1}
.
If we do not care about the dependence, then it may be simplified to the wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
. If the data is supported on a frequency set closed under addition, then we may also restrict the coefficients onto that set.
In this paper, the global wellposedness in a topological space
X
for
t\geq 0
is defined by

(1)

For any
u(0)\in X
, there is a unique global solution
u\in C([0,\infty);X)
.

(2)

For any sequence
\{u_{n}(0)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset X
convergent to some
u_{\infty}(0)\in X
, let
u_{*}\in C([0,\infty);X)
be the corresponding global solutions. Then for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
u_{n}(t)\to u_{\infty}(t)
in
X
uniformly for
0\leq t\leq T
as
n\to\infty
.

The global wellposedness for
t\in\mathbb{R}
is similarly defined, as well as the local one.
The above definition is indeed the standard one in the Hadamard sense when
X
is a metric space.
However, if
X
is not a sequential space, then (2) implies only the sequential continuity for the solution map from the initial data.
Our space
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
is not apparently sequential, in which the topological continuity can be a challenging problem, because the nonlinearity does not preserve the local convexity.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
be a closed subset satisfying
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
.
Suppose that
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
are smooth on a neighborhood of
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
, satisfying
(C-s)
with
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
.
Then the Cauchy problem (
4.4
) is globally wellposed in
(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
for
t\geq 0
.

The simplest choice is
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, then
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{X}
.
As mentioned already, a sufficient condition is that
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}\in Y
(cf. (
2.59
)), and then the global wellposedness holds for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, because the last condition is invariant for the time inversion
t\mapsto-t
.

Proof.

Since the support property is closed in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}\subset\mathcal{X}
is also closed, and it is the inductive limit of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\mu,\nu}
.
The closedness in convolution is immediate from
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
,
while the closedness is obvious for multiplication by the coefficients. Hence all the operations in the construction of solutions, including the iteration argument, are closed in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
so it suffices that the coefficients
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
are defined around
\mathcal{R}
, and we do not need their smoothness near
\xi_{1}=0
.
Moreover,
(C-s)
with
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
implies that (
4.7
) is satisfied for all
\mu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and
t,l>0
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\mu_{0}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{\delta}
, let
\mu:=\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{\delta}(\mu_{0})
.
Then for any
\nu_{0}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and
b_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, by the above result, there exists
\nu\in C([0,\infty);C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0})
such that for any
\hat{u}_{0}\in(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
satisfying
\rho^{\mu,\nu_{0}}(\hat{u}_{0})\leq b_{0}
, there is a unique global solution
u\in C([0,\infty);(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}})
of (
4.6
) satisfying
\rho^{\mu,\nu(t)}(\hat{u}(t))\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty)
.
Thus we obtain a global solution for any initial data
u_{0}\in(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
.

If we have two solutions
u_{1},u_{2}\in C([0,\infty);(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}})
for the same initial data
u_{0}
,
then for any
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\hat{u}_{1}([0,T])\cup\hat{u}_{2}([0,T])\subset(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
is compact, so Proposition
2.10
yields some
\mu_{1},\nu_{1}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and
b_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\rho^{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}(\hat{u}_{j}(t))\leq b_{1}
for
j=1,2
and
t\in[0,T]
.
Then we may apply the uniqueness by contraction for some weight
(\mu_{2},\nu_{2})\geq(\mu_{1},\nu_{1})
and the size
b_{1}
to deduce that
u_{1}=u_{2}
on
t\in[0,T]
. Since
T\in(0,\infty)
is arbitrary, we obtain the uniqueness in
C([0,\infty);(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}})
.

For the sequential continuity of the solution map, take any sequence of initial data
\hat{u}_{0,n}\in(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
convergent to some
\hat{u}_{0,\infty}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
, and let
u_{n}
be the corresponding sequence of solutions.
Since
\{\hat{u}_{0,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\subset(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
is compact,
Proposition
2.10
yields some
\mu_{0},\nu_{0}\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
and
b_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\{\hat{u}_{0,n}\}\subset B_{\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0}}}(0,b_{0}/4)
, and that
\hat{u}_{0,n}\to\hat{u}_{0,\infty}
in
\mathcal{X}^{\mu_{0},\nu_{0},\iota}_{\partial}
for any
\iota\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{+}
.
After replacing
\mu_{0}
with
\mu:=\mathfrak{C}^{1}_{\delta}(\mu_{0})
as before, we use Lemma
2.11
to obtain the weight
\gamma
so that the coefficient multipliers are bounded also on
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu,\gamma\nu}_{\partial}
. For the convolutions in the nonlinearity, we use Lemma
3.5
.
Thus we obtain, in the same way as (
4.20
), for
\hat{u}^{0},\hat{u}^{1}
satisfying
\sup_{t\geq 0}\rho^{\mu,\nu}(\hat{u}^{k})\leq b_{1}
,

\begin{split}&\|\mathcal{D}(\hat{u}_{0}^{0},\hat{u}^{0})-\mathcal{D}(\hat{u}_{0}^{1},\hat{u}^{1})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu,\gamma\nu}_{\partial}}\\
&\quad\leq\rho^{\mu,\nu_{0}}(\hat{u}_{0}^{0}-\hat{u}_{0}^{1})+4\varepsilon b_{1}\tilde{\mathscr{H}}^{(1)}(\varepsilon b_{1})\Gamma(1-\theta)\|\hat{u}^{0}-\hat{u}^{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu,\gamma\nu}_{\partial}},\end{split}

(4.22)

where the extra factor
4
comes from the factor
2
in Lemma
2.11
used twice.
Since
\rho^{\mu,\nu_{0}}(\hat{u}_{0,n})<b_{0}/4
,
the contraction on (
4.21
) implies that
\rho^{\mu,\nu}(\hat{u}_{n})\leq b_{1}/4=b_{0}/2
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
t\geq 0
.
Then (
4.22
) with the smallness of
b_{1}\varepsilon
in (
4.9
) implies

\begin{split}\|\hat{u}_{n}-\hat{u}_{\infty}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu,\gamma\nu}_{\partial}}\leq 2\rho^{\mu,\nu_{0}}(\hat{u}_{0,n}-\hat{u}_{0,\infty})\to 0,\end{split}

(4.23)

as
n\to\infty
.
Combining it with the uniform bound
\rho^{\mu,\nu}(\hat{u}_{n})\leq b_{0}/2
, we obtain by Proposition
2.10
that
\hat{u}_{n}(t)\to\hat{u}_{\infty}(t)
in
\mathcal{X}
locally uniformly for all
t\geq 0
.
∎

A notable special case of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
is when
\mathcal{R}
is a lattice:

\begin{split}\exists L\in(0,\infty)^{d},\quad\mathcal{R}\subset\prod_{1\leq j\leq d}\tfrac{2\pi}{L_{j}}\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

(4.24)

In this case,
\mathcal{R}
should avoid the boundary points on
\xi_{1}=0
, because such distributions do not satisfy the decay condition at
\xi_{1}=0
,
(X-i)
.
Under the above constraint, the solutions in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
are periodic in space: For any
F\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have

\begin{split}F(x_{1}+k_{1}L_{1},\ldots,x_{d}+k_{d}L_{d})=F(x)\end{split}

(4.25)

in the distribution (
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\hat{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}
) sense.

On the other hand, if
F\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
F
is decaying at the spatial infinity in the average sense: By Riemann-Lebesgue,

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni\forall\varphi,\quad\lim_{|x|\to\infty}(\varphi*F)(x)=0.\end{split}

(4.26)

For example, the Littlewood-Paley decomposition of
F
satisfies the decay.
Thus the space
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
contains both the periodic solutions and the decaying solutions, which
is in a sharp contrast with the usual settings for the Cauchy problem, where the boundary condition at infinity has to be distinguished by the solution space.

The condition
(C-s)
requires uniform boundedness of the coefficients
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}
in the orthogonal directions.
For much more general equations, it may be satisfied by restricting
\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}_{0}
in the orthogonal directions, which is the case if
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
is bounded for each
l\in(0,\infty)
, then

(C-0)
implies
(C-s)
with
\theta=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus Corollary
4.2
implies Theorem
1.2
.
Note that in this case (
4.4
) makes sense in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
, with the classical time derivative in the topology of
\mathcal{X}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
is a bit simplified:

Lemma 4.3
.

If
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is closed such that
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
is bounded for each
l>0
, then

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}=\{F\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathcal{R},\ X^{l}F\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\text{ for some $l>0$}\}.\end{split}

(4.27)

If in addition
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}=\varnothing
for some
l>0
, then
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}=\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

In particular in the periodic case,
\mathcal{R}
can not contain any point on the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
as mentioned above, so the last statement holds.
(
4.27
) applies to
\mathcal{X}
itself if
d=1
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
2.4
, it suffices to derive the condition
(X-ii)
from the other conditions.
For any
l\in(0,\infty)
, the boundedness of
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
with the definition of
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
implies that
F
is of finite order on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
. Hence there exists
m(l)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|F(\varphi)|\leq m(l)\|\varphi\|_{m(l)}=m(l)\|\varphi\|_{m(l)}^{+}
for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{(0,l)}
, which is enough for the condition
(X-ii)
, by the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
The last sentence of lemma is obvious.
∎

Examples of
\mathcal{R}
satisfying the local boundedness
are conical regions in the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}:=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|\xi^{\perp}|\leq R(\xi_{1})\},\end{split}

(4.28)

for some
R\in C^{+}_{0}
that is super-additive (
R(a)+R(b)\leq R(a+b)
for all
a,b\geq 0
),
which implies
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
.
The left side
|\xi^{\perp}|
may be replaced with any semi-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that bounds
\xi^{\perp}
.
We may choose
R
to grow arbitrarily fast by

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
d\in\mathbb{N}
, any non-empty
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
and any locally bounded
R_{0}:A\to[0,\infty)
satisfying

\begin{split}\limsup_{\xi\in A,\ |\xi|\to+0}|\xi|^{-1}R_{0}(\xi)<\infty,\end{split}

(4.29)

there exists a super-additive
R\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
satisfying
R_{0}(\xi)\leq R(|\xi|)
for all
\xi\in A
.

Proof.

Let
R_{1}(l):=\sup\{|\eta|^{-1}R_{0}(\eta)\mid\eta\in A,\ 0<|\eta|\leq l\}
.
Then
R_{1}:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by the assumptions, and it is non-decreasing. Then
R:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
defined by

R(l):=\int_{l}^{2l}R_{1}(t)dt

belongs to
C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
, satisfying

\begin{split}R(a+b)\geq\int_{a+b}^{2a+b}R_{1}(t-b)dt+\int_{2a+b}^{2a+2b}R_{1}(t-2a)dt=R(a)+R(b)\end{split}

(4.30)

and
R(|\xi|)\geq|\xi|R_{1}(|\xi|)\geq R_{0}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in A
.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

In addition to the translation invariance, the space
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
is invariant for the exponential Fourier multipliers in the form
e^{z\partial_{1}}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}e^{iz\xi_{1}}\mathcal{F}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
which is the holomorphic extension for the
x_{1}
variable.
Indeed, the multiplication with
e^{iz\xi_{1}}
is bounded
\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu_{0}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\mu,\nu_{1}}
if
e^{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|l}\nu_{0}(l)\leq\nu_{1}(l)
for all
l>0
, so it is continuous on
\mathcal{X}
.
Moreover, the convolution is invariant: for any
F,G\in\mathcal{X}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\begin{split}e^{iz\xi_{1}}(F*G)=(e^{iz\xi_{1}}F)*(e^{iz\xi_{1}}G),\end{split}

(4.31)

which is immediate for
F,G\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and then extended by the density of
\mathscr{D}_{>0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
, Proposition
2.6
.
Hence the set of solutions has the same invariance: If
u\in C(I;(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X})^{n_{1}})
is a solution of (
1.1
) on an interval
I
, then
e^{z\partial_{1}}u\in C(I;(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X})^{n_{1}})
is also a solution for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

5.
Comparison with other solutions

Although our definition of solutions is quite natural in view of the Fourier transform of the equation, it is non-trivial to compare those solutions with others defined and constructed in different ways.
The bottleneck to use the uniqueness in
\mathcal{X}
is the Fourier support condition and decay at
\xi_{1}=0
.

Nevertheless, the following is a natural procedure to compare our solutions in
\mathcal{X}
with some others, when it is easy to obtain the latter.
Suppose

(1)

Global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
holds (Corollary
4.2
) for some
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the closure of the interior of
\mathcal{R}
contains
0
.

(2)

Local wellposedness holds in another space
X
such that
\mathscr{D}_{>0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}X\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(3)

I_{n}
in Proposition
2.6
is an approximating sequence also on
X
.

Then we may choose the approximating sequence
I_{n}
of Proposition
2.6
such that
I_{n}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}\to\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
For any initial data
u_{0}\in(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}
, we have an approximating sequence
u_{0,n}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}I_{n}\hat{u}_{0}\in(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}}\hookrightarrow X^{n_{1}}
.
Let
u_{n}
be the solution for the initial data
u_{0,n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
implies that
u_{n}\to u
in
C([0,\infty);(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}})^{n_{1}})
.
The local wellposedness in
X
implies that
u_{n}\in C([0,T_{n});X^{n_{1}})
for the maximal existence time
T_{n}\in(0,\infty]
.
If
u_{0}\in X^{n_{1}}
, then we have
u_{0,n}\to u_{0}
by the approximation property in
X
, so the local wellposedness in
X
implies
u_{n}\to u
in
C([0,T);X^{n_{1}})
, where
T\in(0,\infty]
is the maximal time of
u
.
Here the limit
u
is the same, as long as the convergence holds, because both of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{F}X
are continuously embedded into
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Therefore, the solutions are the same between
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
X
, for all the initial data in their intersection as long as the solution exists in
X
.
In this sense, we may regard the solutions in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
as extension from
X\cap\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
However, unless the global wellposedness holds also in
X
, the maximal existence time
T_{n}
may well go to
0
as
n\to\infty
when
u_{0}\not\in X^{n_{1}}
.

5.1.
Solutions beyond blow-up

Blow-up may well happen in finite time in
X
, while the solution is extended globally in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
A typical example is the ODE

\begin{split}\dot{u}=u^{2},\end{split}

(5.1)

whose Cauchy problem is explicitly solved with the Taylor expansion:

\begin{split}u(t)=\tfrac{u_{0}}{1-tu_{0}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}t^{n}u_{0}^{n+1},\end{split}

(5.2)

whose radius of convergence is
|tu_{0}|<1
with the simple pole at
tu_{0}=1
.
The iteration sequence
\{v_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
defined by

\begin{split}\dot{v}_{n}=v_{n-1}^{2},\quad v_{0}(0)=u_{0},\end{split}

(5.3)

is in the form (which may be shown by induction)

\begin{split}v_{n}(t)=\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2^{n}-1}c_{n,k}t^{k}u_{0}^{k+1},\quad\begin{cases}c_{n,k}=1&(0\leq k\leq n)\\
0<c_{n,k}<1&(n<k\leq 2^{n}-1).\end{cases}\end{split}

(5.4)

Using
\|f*g\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{M}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for the Fourier transform of the equation,
it is easy to see that this equation is locally wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
,
and the iteration converges to
u
for
|t|\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}<1
, where
\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}:=\|\mathcal{F}\varphi\|_{\mathcal{M}}
.
Moreover, if
\hat{u}_{0}
is a non-negative measure,
then we have
\|\hat{u}_{0}*\hat{u}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\hat{u}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

and similarly for iterations, so

\begin{split}\|u(t)\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}t^{n}\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}^{n+1}=\tfrac{\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}}{1-t\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}}\end{split}

(5.5)

blows up exactly as
t\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}\to 1-0
.
Since the non-negative Fourier transform implies

\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=u(t,0)=\|u(t)\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}
,
it also blows up in
L^{\infty}_{x}
and at
x=0
, as well as in smaller Banach algebras, such as
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
for
s>d/2
.
On the other hand,
u(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{n}u_{0}^{n+1}
is convergent and smooth for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
in
\mathcal{X}
.
All the above remains valid when
\mathcal{M}
is replaced with the closed subspace
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

A more interesting case is the Burgers equation

\begin{split}u_{t}=u_{xx}+2uu_{x}\end{split}

(5.6)

for
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+1}\to\mathbb{C}
, which is well-known to be explicitly solvable by the Cole-Hopf transform, thereby the blow-up has been intensively studied, cf.
[
30
]
for the complex-valued blow-up solutions on
\mathbb{R}
.
A simple example within our function space is for the monochromatic initial data

\begin{split}u_{0}(x)=-iae^{ix},\end{split}

(5.7)

with a parameter
a\in\mathbb{C}
, for which
[
19
]
proved blow-up in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Here we investigate the unique global solution
u\in C(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X})
(given by Corollary
4.2
), using the explicit formula in the Fourier space as in
[
19
]
:

\begin{split}&u(t)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}u^{n}(t)e^{inx},\quad u^{1}(t)=-iae^{-t},\\
&u^{n}(t)=in\int_{0}^{t}e^{-n^{2}(t-s)}\sum_{1\leq k\leq n-1}u^{k}(s)u^{n-k}(s)ds\quad(n\geq 2).\end{split}

(5.8)

Changing the variable from
u^{n}
to
U_{n}
defined by

\begin{split}u^{n}(t)=-i(ae^{-t})^{n}U_{n}(t),\end{split}

(5.9)

the equation may be rewritten as

\begin{split}U_{1}(t)=1,\quad U_{n}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-n(n-1)(t-s)}n\sum_{1\leq k\leq n-1}U_{k}(s)U_{n-k}(s)ds.\end{split}

(5.10)

Then we easily observe by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
that

\begin{split}U_{2}(t)^{n-1}=(1-e^{-2t})^{n-1}\leq U_{n}(t)\leq 1.\end{split}

(5.11)

Indeed, it is obviously satisfied for
n=1,2
, while for
n\geq 3
, the upper bound is immediate by injecting the induction hypothesis for
k=1,\ldots,n-1
into the equation.
For the lower bound, the same injection yields

\begin{split}U_{n}(t)\geq e^{-n(n-1)t}\int_{0}^{t}n(n-1)e^{n(n-1)s}U_{2}(s)^{n-2}ds,\end{split}

(5.12)

and the conclusion follows by replacing the integrand with

\begin{split}\partial_{s}[e^{n(n-1)s}U_{2}(s)^{n-1}]&=n(n-1)e^{n(n-1)s}U_{2}(s)^{n-2}(1-\tfrac{n-2}{n}e^{-2s})\\
&\leq n(n-1)e^{n(n-1)s}U_{2}(s)^{n-2}.\end{split}

(5.13)

For the original Fourier coefficients, we thus obtain

\begin{split}|a|e^{-t}\cdot|ae^{-t}(1-e^{-2t})|^{n-1}\leq|u^{n}(t)|=|ae^{-t}|^{n}U_{n}(t)\leq|ae^{-t}|^{n}.\end{split}

(5.14)

Note that
e^{-t}(1-e^{-2t})
attains its maximum
\tfrac{2}{3\sqrt{3}}
at
t=\tfrac{1}{2}\log 3
.

On the other hand, the equation is locally wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
, where the above solution
u
satisfies

\begin{split}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|u^{n}(t)|=\|u(t)\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}=iu(t,x_{a})=\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}},\end{split}

(5.15)

for all
x_{a}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
ae^{ix}=|a|
.
The classical blow-up occurs when the above diverges.
Hence the threshold on
|a|
for blow-up is given by

\begin{split}&\mathfrak{B}:=\{(t,a)\in[0,\infty)^{2}\mid\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(ae^{-t})^{n}U_{n}(t)=\infty\},\\
&a_{*}(t):=\inf\{a\mid(t,a)\in\mathfrak{B}\},\quad a_{**}:=\inf_{t\geq 0}a_{*}(t).\end{split}

(5.16)

(
5.14
) implies

\begin{split}e^{t}\leq a_{*}(t)\leq\tfrac{e^{t}}{1-e^{-2t}},\quad 1\leq a_{**}\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\sqrt{3}=\inf_{t\geq 0}\tfrac{e^{t}}{1-e^{-2t}},\end{split}

(5.17)

while the local wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
implies
\lim_{t\to+0}a_{*}(t)=\infty
.
The classical blow-up time is given by

\begin{split}T_{*}(a):=T_{*}(|a|)=\inf\{t>0\mid a_{*}(t)\leq|a|\}>0\quad(\inf\varnothing=\infty).\end{split}

(5.18)

Indeed, for
0\leq t<T_{*}(a)
,
|a|<a_{*}(t)
implies that
u^{n}(t)
is exponentially decaying as
n\to\infty
, so
u(t)
is analytic.
If
u(T_{*}(a))\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
, then the local wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
implies that
u\in C([0,T];\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M})
for some
T>T_{*}(a)
, and also for slightly bigger
|a|
by the continuous dependence on
u(0)
, contradicting the definition of
T_{*}(a)
.
Hence, if
T_{*}(a)<\infty
, then
(T_{*}(a),|a|)\in\mathfrak{B}
and
T_{*}(a)=\min\{t\mid(t,|a|)\in\mathfrak{B}\}
, so
a_{*}(T_{*}(a))\leq|a|
, while
T_{*}(a_{*}(t))\leq t
is obvious by definition.
Then (
5.14
) implies

\begin{split}e^{T_{*}(a)}\leq|a|\leq\tfrac{e^{T_{*}(a)}}{1-e^{-2T_{*}(a)}}.\end{split}

(5.19)

In particular
T_{*}(a)\leq\log|a|
. It is obvious by definition that
T_{*}(a)=\infty
for
|a|<a_{**}
and
T_{*}(a)<\infty
for
|a|>a_{**}
.
Let
T_{**}:=T_{*}(a_{**})
.
If
T_{**}=\infty
then by the local wellposedness as above, the solution with slightly bigger
|a|
exists for
t<\log a_{**}+1
, contradicting
T_{*}(a)\leq\log|a|
for
|a|>a_{**}
.
Hence
(T_{**},a_{**})\in\mathfrak{B}
,
a_{*}(T_{**})=a_{**}=\min a_{*}
, and

u
blows up iff
|a|\geq a_{**}
, and at
t=T_{*}(a)\leq\log|a|
.
Since
T_{*}
is non-increasing by definition, the blow-up time is bounded for all
a\in\mathbb{C}
by

\begin{split}\max\{T_{*}(a)\mid a\in\mathbb{C},\ T_{*}(a)<\infty\}=T_{*}(a_{**})\leq\log|a_{**}|.\end{split}

(5.20)

If
a_{*}(t)<|a|
(which happens iff
|a|>a_{**}
),
u^{n}(t)
is exponentially divergent as
n\to\infty
(along a subsequence) so
u(t)\not\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.
There can be some time gap after
T_{*}(a)
if
a_{*}(t)\geq|a|
on some time interval starting from
T_{*}(a)
, but such intervals are mutually disjoint, so those exceptional
|a|
are at most countable. Thus we obtain

Proposition 5.1
.

There exist unique
a_{**}\in(1,\tfrac{3}{2}\sqrt{3}]
,
T_{**}\in(0,\log\tfrac{3}{2}\sqrt{3})
and
a_{*}:(0,\infty)\to[a_{**},\infty)
, satisfying
\lim_{t\to+0}a_{*}(t)=\infty
, (
5.17
) and
\min a_{*}=a_{**}=a_{*}(T_{**})
, such that
the unique solution
u\in C(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X})
of (
5.6
)–(
5.7
) blows up in the classical sense if and only if
|a|\geq a_{**}
and at
T_{*}(a):=\min a_{*}^{-1}((0,|a|])\leq\min(T_{**},\log|a|)
.
For those
t>0
where
|a|<a_{*}(t)
,
u(t)
is analytic. For
|a|>a_{*}(t)
,
u^{n}(t)
is exponentially divergent as
n\to\infty
and so
u(t)\not\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.
Excepting a countable set of
|a|
,
u(t)
gets out of
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
immediately after
T_{*}(a)
, while
u(t)
is analytic for
t<T_{*}(a)
and for

t>T^{*}(a):=\sup a_{*}^{-1}((0,|a|])\leq\log|a|
.

Remark 5.1
.

The above result may look contradictory with
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]
, which reads blow-up at arbitrarily large time, but what is actually proven in
[
19
]
is that

\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})}\sim\|u^{n}(t)\|_{\ell^{2}_{n}(\mathbb{N})}=\infty
for
T_{0}:=\sum_{k\geq 2}\tfrac{1}{k^{2}}\log\tfrac{3k-3}{2k-3}\leq t\leq\log\tfrac{|a|}{3}
, as well as exponential divergence of
u^{n}(t)
for
T_{0}\leq t<\log\tfrac{|a|}{3}
.
The above result shows that the (first) blow-up time is earlier (which is also obvious from the argument in
[
19
]
), and uniformly bounded by
T_{**}
. Indeed,
3e^{t}>\tfrac{e^{t}}{1-e^{-2t}}
for
t>T_{1}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\tfrac{3}{2}
and (
5.17
) imply
|a|>a_{*}(t)
for
T_{1}<t\leq\log\tfrac{|a|}{3}
, while
T_{1}<\tfrac{1}{4}\log 3<T_{0}
.
Since the inverse function of
\tau:(\tfrac{1}{2}\log 3,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\tfrac{e^{t}}{1-e^{-2t}}
satisfies
\tau^{-1}(a)=\log a-a^{-2}+O(a^{-4})
as
a\to\infty
,
the time interval
[T_{0},\log\tfrac{|a|}{3}]
considered in
[
19
]
is inside the set where
u(t)\not\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
with a uniform distance from the boundary.

Remark 5.2
.

The Cole-Hopf transform yields another solution
\tilde{u}
by the formula

\begin{split}v:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\tfrac{a^{n}}{n!}e^{inx-n^{2}t},\quad\tilde{u}:=-v_{x}/v.\end{split}

(5.21)

The blow-up occurs at
v(t,x)=0
, before which
\tilde{u}
is analytic and the same as the solution in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
, namely
\tilde{u}|_{0\leq t<T_{*}(a)}=u|_{0\leq t<T_{*}(a)}
.

To see what happens after that, let
V(t,x):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\tfrac{1}{n!}e^{inx-n^{2}t}
, which is analytic on
H:=(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{C}
satisfying the heat equation and
v(t,x)=V(t,x-i\log a)
.
Let
Z:=\{(t,x)\in H\mid V(t,x)=0\}
and
D:=\{(t,x)\in Z\mid V_{x}(t,x)=0\}
.
Then

[
29
, Theorem 4.1]
implies that
D
is discrete in
H
.
For any
(t_{0},x_{0})\in Z\setminus D
, the Weierstrass preparation theorem yields analytic functions
z(t),A(t,x)
defined around
(t,x)=(t_{0},x_{0})
such that

\begin{split}V(t,x)=A(t,x)(x-x_{0}-z(t)),\quad z(t_{0})=0,\quad A(t_{0},x_{0})\not=0.\end{split}

(5.22)

Let
E
be the set of all
(t_{0},x_{0})\in Z\setminus D
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t_{0}-\varepsilon)\not=0\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t_{0}-\varepsilon)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t_{0}+\varepsilon)
for all small
\varepsilon>0
. Then the analyticity of
z
implies existence of a positive even integer
m
such that

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\partial_{t}^{k}z(t_{0})=0\not=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\partial_{t}^{m}z(t_{0})
for
k<m
.
Since
Z=\{(t,x_{0}+z(t))\}
in a neighborhood of
(t_{0},x_{0})
,
we deduce that
E
is also discrete in
H
.
On the other hand,
V(0,x)=e^{e^{ix}}
has no zero for
x\in\mathbb{C}
,
V(t,x)\to 1
as
t\to\infty
uniformly for bounded
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x
, and
|V(t,x)-1|\leq e^{e^{-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x}}-1
.
Hence for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, there is
\delta>0
such that for any
(t,x)\in Z
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x>\lambda
we have
\delta\leq t\leq 1/\delta
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x\leq 1/\delta
.
Therefore,

\begin{split}S_{\lambda}:=\{(t,x)\in D\cup E\mid 0\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}x\leq 2\pi,\ \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x>\lambda\}\end{split}

(5.23)

is finite for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
Hence
A:=\{e^{-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}x}\mid(t,x)\in S_{-\infty}\}\subset(0,\infty)
is locally finite.

Now let
a\in\mathbb{C}
satisfy
|a|\geq a_{**}
and
|a|\not\in A
.
Then
t_{0}:=T_{*}(a)\in(0,T_{**}]
is the first
t>0
such that
0\in v(t,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
\mathcal{B}(a):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid v(t_{0},x)=0\}
be the blow-up set,
x_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(a)
and
x_{0}:=x_{1}-i\log a
.
Then
(t_{0},x_{0})\in Z\setminus(D\cup E)
.
Let
z(t)
as in (
5.22
).
Since
0\not\in v(t,\mathbb{R})
for any
t\in(0,t_{0})
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)\not=0
for
t<t_{0}
.
Since
(t_{0},x_{0})\not\in E
, the minimal integer
m
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\partial_{t}^{m}z(t_{0})\not=0
is odd.
In other words, the blow-up for
|a|\not\in A
occurs when some zeros of
v
crosses
\mathbb{R}
in
\mathbb{C}
. It also implies that
\mathcal{B}(a)
is discrete in
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{B}(a)\cap[0,2\pi)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{J}\}
such that all
x_{j}
are distinct.
Then we have an open interval
I\ni t_{0}
and analytic functions
z_{j}(t)
defined on
I
for
j=1,\ldots,J
such that
v(t,x)=A_{j}(t,x)(x-x_{j}-z_{j}(t))
around
(t_{0},x_{j})
, for some analytic
A_{j}
satisfying
A_{j}(t_{0},x_{j})\not=0
. Then there exists
\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{J}\}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\begin{split}R(t,x):=\tilde{u}(t,x)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}[e^{-i(x-x_{j}-z_{j}(t))}-1]^{-1}\end{split}

(5.24)

is analytic for
(t,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}
, and that each
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{j}(t)
changes its sign at
t=t_{0}
.
The last summand may be expanded by using

\begin{split}[e^{-i(x-z)}-1]^{-1}=\begin{cases}\sum_{n\geq 1}e^{in(x-z)}&(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z<0),\\
-\sum_{n\leq 0}e^{in(x-z)}&(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z>0).\end{cases}\end{split}

(5.25)

Since
\tilde{u}
is analytic for
t<t_{0}
and
\operatorname{supp}\mathcal{F}\tilde{u}\subset[1,\infty)
,
we deduce that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{j}(t)<0
for
t<t_{0}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z_{j}(t)>0
for
t>t_{0}
, for all
j
. Therefore

\begin{split}\tilde{u}(t,x)=\begin{cases}\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}\sum_{n\geq 1}e^{in(x-x_{j}-z_{j}(t))}+R(t,x)&(t_{0}-\varepsilon<t<t_{0})\\
\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}\sum_{n\leq 0}e^{in(x-x_{j}-z_{j}(t))}+R(t,x)&(t_{0}<t<t_{0}+\varepsilon).\end{cases}\end{split}

(5.26)

In particular,
\tilde{u}(t)
is discontinuous even in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
at the blow-up time
t=t_{0}=T_{*}(a)
.

The above construction of blow-up works partly for the equation

\begin{split}u_{t}=\Delta u+2u\cdot\nabla u,\quad u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}^{d},\end{split}

(5.27)

with the general dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
and the initial data in the form

\begin{split}u_{0}=-a\nabla\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mu,\quad a>0,\quad 0\leq\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\operatorname{supp}\mu\subset B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathrm{v},R),\end{split}

(5.28)

for some
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and
R\in(0,|\mathrm{v}|/\sqrt{2})
.
Then the iteration

\begin{split}&u(t)=-\nabla\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}a^{n}\mathcal{F}^{-1}U_{n}(t),\quad U_{1}(t,\xi)=e^{-t|\xi|^{2}}\mu(\xi),\\
&U_{n}(t,\xi)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq n-1}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\xi-\eta)\cdot\eta U_{k}(s,\xi-\eta)U_{n-k}(s,\eta)d\eta ds,\end{split}

(5.29)

gives the unique global solution
u\in C(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v}))
with

\operatorname{supp}U_{n}\subset nB_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathrm{v},R)
.
Let

\begin{split}&\underline{b}:=\inf_{\xi\in\operatorname{supp}\mu}|\xi|\geq|\mathrm{v}|-R>0,\quad\bar{b}:=\sup_{\xi\in\operatorname{supp}\mu}|\xi|\in[\underline{b},|\mathrm{v}|+R],\\
&\underline{c}:=\inf_{\xi,\eta\in\operatorname{supp}\mu}\xi\cdot\eta\geq\min(1-\tfrac{R^{2}}{|\mathrm{v}|^{2}},\tfrac{1}{2})(|\mathrm{v}|^{2}-2R^{2})>0,\\
&\bar{c}:=\sup_{\xi,\eta\in\operatorname{supp}\mu}\xi\cdot\eta\in[\bar{c},\bar{b}^{2}].\end{split}

(5.30)

Then the equation of
U_{n}
is bounded from below and above by

\begin{split}&\tfrac{1}{n}e^{-\bar{b}^{2}nt}\underline{f}_{n}(t)\mu^{*n}\leq U_{n}(t)\leq\tfrac{1}{n}e^{-\underline{b}^{2}nt}\bar{f}_{n}(t)\mu^{*n},\quad\underline{f}_{1}(t):=\bar{f}_{1}(t):=1,\\
&\quad\underline{f}_{n}(t):=\underline{c}\int_{0}^{t}ne^{-\bar{b}^{2}n(n-1)(t-s)}\sum_{1\leq k\leq n-1}\underline{f}_{k}(s)\underline{f}_{n-k}(s)ds,\\
&\quad\bar{f}_{n}(t):=\bar{c}\int_{0}^{t}ne^{-\underline{b}^{2}n(n-1)(t-s)}\sum_{1\leq k\leq n-1}\bar{f}_{k}(s)\bar{f}_{n-k}(s)ds,\end{split}

(5.31)

by induction on
n
. Then in the same way as before, we obtain inductively

\begin{split}&(\bar{b}^{-2}\underline{c}(1-e^{-2\bar{b}^{2}t}))^{n-1}\leq\underline{f}_{n}(t)\leq\bar{f}_{n}(t)\leq(\underline{b}^{-2}\bar{c})^{n-1}.\end{split}

(5.32)

The lower bound implies exponential growth of
\|a^{n}U_{n}(t)\|_{\mathcal{M}}
as
n\to\infty
if

\begin{split}a>\tfrac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\bar{b}^{2}\underline{c}^{-1},\end{split}

(5.33)

at some
t\in(0,\infty)
, where
(\tfrac{3\sqrt{3}}{2})^{-1}=\max_{t>0}e^{-\bar{b}^{2}t}(1-e^{-2\bar{b}^{2}t})
as before. By the Fourier support and positivity, it implies that
u(t)\not\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for those
t>0
.
On the other hand, the upper bound implies that
\|a^{n}U_{n}(t)\|_{\mathcal{M}}
decays exponentially as
n\to\infty
if

\begin{split}0\leq a<\underline{b}^{2}\bar{c}^{-1},\end{split}

(5.34)

uniformly in
t\geq 0
, and so
u
is an analytic global solution on
t\geq 0
.

The above examples show that the global wellposedness without size restriction as in this paper can not hold within the tempered distributions
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
,
even with a smoothing linear part, a very mild nonlinearity, and analytic initial data.

5.2.
Solutions beyond the natural boundary

For the ODE
\dot{u}=u^{2}
, the solution formula (
5.2
) makes sense and smooth except at most the single time
t=1/u_{0}
for each
u_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
.
However, the global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
applies to more exotic equations as well, for example

\begin{split}\dot{u}=\mathscr{H}(u):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u^{2^{n}}.\end{split}

(5.35)

This
\mathscr{H}(u)
is a well-known lacunary series with the natural boundary
|u|=1
,
so it may not be extended anywhere of
|u|\geq 1
as a holomorphic function.
Nevertheless, the global wellposedness holds in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
, which includes initial data such as

\begin{split}u_{0}(x)=ae^{ic\cdot x},\end{split}

(5.36)

for any
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
.
If we choose
|a|\geq 1
, then
\mathscr{H}(u_{0}(x))
does not make sense for the holomorphic function
\mathscr{H}
at any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
On the other hand, the local wellposedness holds for
\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}<1
and obtained by the iteration estimate as above, where the solutions are the same as those in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
.
In this sense, the global solutions in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
may be regarded as extension beyond the analytic continuation on
\mathbb{C}
.
Actually, we could also consider everywhere divergent Taylor series if the initial Fourier support is uniformly away from the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
, but we do not pursue it here.

6.
Optimality of the conditions for the solution space

In this section, we investigate the optimality of the function space
\mathcal{X}
and the stronger support condition for the global wellposedness results, using the simple ODE (
5.1
) with the solution formula (
5.2
).

6.1.
Frequency on the half space

The first example shows that

\hat{u}_{0}
is not allowed to have point measures either in negative frequency
\xi_{1}<0
or on the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
, for global existence in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
.
This means that the half space is optimal for the Fourier support condition, including the zero boundary condition.

Let
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
u
be the solution of (
5.1
) for the initial data

\begin{split}u(0,x)=\cos(c\cdot x),\quad\hat{u}(0,\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}[\delta(\xi-c)+\delta(\xi+c)].\end{split}

(6.1)

Note that for a single point measure
\delta(x-c)
with
c\not=0
, we may choose the direction
\mathrm{v}=c
to satisfy
\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}(\mathrm{v})
and so the global wellposedness follows.

Now we confirm blow-up of the above solution
u
in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Although it may look obvious from the solution formula, the previous section suggests that such very weak solutions might potentially survive beyond our intuition.
The Cauchy problem is locally wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
, where we have

\begin{split}u(t,x)=\tfrac{\cos(c\cdot x)}{1-t\cos(c\cdot x)}=\sum_{n\geq 0}t^{n}\cos^{n+1}(c\cdot x),\end{split}

(6.2)

which is absolutely convergent in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M}
for
|t|<1
and
u\in C((-1,1);\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{M})
.
If
c\not=0
then the Fourier transform is in the form

\begin{split}\hat{u}(t,\xi)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{j,n}t^{n}\delta(\xi-jc),\end{split}

(6.3)

for certain non-negative coefficients
a_{j,n}
that may be computed explicitly. In particular at
\xi=0
, the binomial expansion of
\cos^{n+1}(c\cdot x)=(\tfrac{e^{icx}+e^{-icx}}{2})^{n+1}
yields

\begin{split}\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{0,n}t^{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\begin{pmatrix}2k\\
k\end{pmatrix}\tfrac{t^{2k-1}}{2^{2k}}\geq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\tfrac{t^{2k-1}}{2k}\to\infty,\end{split}

(6.4)

as
0<t\to 1-0
. The above inequality follows from

\tfrac{a_{0,2k+1}}{a_{0,2k-1}}=\tfrac{2k+1}{2k+2}\geq\tfrac{k}{k+1}
and
a_{0,1}=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Hence, coupling
\hat{u}(t)
with a test function
\phi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with small support around
\xi=0
, we deduce that
u(t)
blows up in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
t\to 1-0
.
In the case of
c=0
, it is more direct from the formula
\hat{u}(t,\xi)=\tfrac{1}{1-t}\delta(\xi)
.

6.2.
Decay to the boundary

For finite measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, the vanishing at the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
is equivalent to the decay as
\xi_{1}\to+0
.
It is the case for elements in
\mathcal{X}
, because they are required to be
\mathbb{C}
-valued measures near
\xi_{1}=0
.
Then it is natural to ask if the latter condition is necessary.
In other words, can we weaken or replace the topology for the decay as
\xi_{1}\to+0
, or can we include
\hat{u}_{0}
that is not a measure near
\xi_{1}=0
?
The following shows that the measure space is optimal at least among the family of Sobolev or Besov spaces (in the Fourier side). Let

\begin{split}B^{-0}:=\bigcap_{\sigma<0}(B^{\sigma}_{1,\infty}\cap B^{\sigma}_{\infty,\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\end{split}

(6.5)

be the Fréchet space endowed with a countable family of norms, e.g.,
\|\varphi\|_{B^{-1/n}_{1,\infty}}+\|\varphi\|_{B^{-1/n}_{\infty,\infty}}
or
\|{\langle\nabla\rangle}^{-1/n}\varphi\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It is also a convolution algebra because

\begin{split}\|f*g\|_{B^{2\sigma}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{\sigma}_{1,1}}\|g\|_{B^{\sigma}_{p,1}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{\sigma/2}_{1,\infty}}(\|g\|_{B^{\sigma/2}_{1,\infty}}+\|g\|_{B^{\sigma/2}_{\infty,\infty}}),\end{split}

(6.6)

for any
\sigma<0
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, but we can not solve (
5.1
) in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}B^{-0}_{\geq 0}
.
More precisely,

Proposition 6.1
.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{D}_{>0}
convergent in
B^{-0}
such that the global solution
u_{n}\in C(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X})
of (
5.1
) with
\hat{u}_{n}(0)=f_{n}
is unbounded in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
at any fixed
t\not=0
.
The iteration sequence
v_{n}
defined by (
5.3
) with
\hat{u}_{0}=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}
is also unbounded at any fixed
t\not=0
.

Note that the convergence from
\mathscr{D}_{>0}
implies the decay of the limit
\hat{u}_{0}
to the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
in the
B^{-0}
norm.
So we may not include any negative Sobolev or Besov space up to the boundary
\xi_{1}=0
, for the local wellposedness, or to construct the solution by iteration.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

For
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\begin{split}&r_{k}:=1/k!,\quad N_{k}:=k^{k^{2}},\quad M_{k}:=2^{k^{2}},\\
&g_{k}:=\chi(4r_{k+1}^{-1}|\xi-r_{k}e_{1}|)\exp(-iN_{k}\xi_{1}),\quad f_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{k}g_{k}.\end{split}

(6.7)

Then
\operatorname{supp}g_{k}\subset\{|\xi-r_{k}e_{1}|<r_{k+1}/2\}
implies
g_{k},f_{n}\in\mathscr{D}_{>0}
, and we have

\begin{split}&\check{g}_{k}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}g_{k}=[r_{k+1}^{d}\check{\chi}(r_{k+1}x)e^{ir_{k}x_{1}}]|_{x\mapsto x-N_{k}e_{1}},\end{split}

(6.8)

where
\check{\chi}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\chi(4|\xi|)
, and

\begin{split}\|{\langle\nabla\rangle}^{-\varepsilon}g_{k}\|_{(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})_{\xi}}\lesssim\|(1-\Delta)^{d}[{\langle x\rangle}^{-\varepsilon}\check{g}_{k}](x+N_{k}e_{1})\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim N_{k}^{-\varepsilon},\end{split}

(6.9)

uniformly for
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
M_{k}/N_{k}^{\varepsilon}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
for any
\varepsilon>0
,
f_{n}
is convergent in
B^{-0}
.

The global solution
u_{n}
is given by the formula

\hat{u}_{n}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t^{j-1}f_{n}^{*j}
as before,
and the support of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
implies that for any
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}_{<r_{n-1}-r_{n}/2}
we have

\begin{split}\hat{u}_{n}(t)(\varphi)=\sum_{1\leq j<s_{n}}t^{j-1}(M_{n}g_{n})^{*j}(\varphi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}t^{j-1}(M_{n}\check{g}_{n})^{j}(\hat{\varphi}),\end{split}

(6.10)

where
s_{n}:=\tfrac{r_{n-1}-r_{n}/2}{r_{n}-r_{n+1}/2}=n-\tfrac{1}{2n+1}
.
Since
(r_{n}+\tfrac{1}{2}r_{n+1})(n-1)<r_{n-1}-\tfrac{1}{2}r_{n}
, we may take
\varphi=\varphi_{n}:=(\bar{g}_{n})^{*(n-1)}
, then

\begin{split}(2\pi)^{d}\hat{u}_{n}(t)(\varphi_{n})&=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}t^{j-1}M_{n}^{j}{\langle\check{g}_{n}^{j}|\check{g}_{n}^{n-1}\rangle}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\end{split}

(6.11)

where the summand for
j=n-1
is equal to

\begin{split}t^{n-2}M_{n}^{n-1}\|r_{n+1}^{d}\check{\chi}(r_{n+1}x)\|_{L^{2(n-1)}}^{2(n-1)}=t^{n-2}M_{n}^{n-1}r_{n+1}^{d(2n-3)}\|\check{\chi}\|_{L^{2n-2}}^{2n-2},\end{split}

(6.12)

while for
j<n-1
it is bounded by

\begin{split}t^{j-1}M_{n}^{j}r_{n+1}^{d(n+j-2)}\|\check{\chi}\|_{L^{n+j-1}}^{n+j-1}.\end{split}

(6.13)

Since
\check{\chi}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\check{\chi}(0)>0
, there are
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\begin{split}c_{0}c_{1}^{p}\leq\|\check{\chi}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\leq c_{2}c_{3}^{p}\end{split}

(6.14)

for all
p\in[1,\infty)
. Since
M_{n}r_{n+1}^{d}=2^{n^{2}}((n+1)!)^{-d}\gg A^{n}
as
n\to\infty
for any
A\in(1,\infty)
,
we deduce that

\begin{split}|\hat{u}_{n}(t)(\varphi_{n})|\gtrsim t^{n-2}M_{n}^{n-1}r_{n+1}^{d(2n-3)}c_{1}^{2n-2}\geq t^{-1}(c_{1}^{2}tM_{n}r_{n+1}^{2d})^{n-1}\end{split}

(6.15)

for sufficiently large
n
(depending on
d,t
).

On the other hand, since
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{n}\subset\{|\xi|<2(n-1)r_{n}\}\subset\{|\xi|\leq 2\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\hat{u}_{n}(t)
is bounded in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for large
n

\begin{split}|\hat{u}_{n}(t)(\varphi_{n})|\leq m\|\varphi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{m}}\leq m\|g_{n}\|_{L^{1}}^{n-2}\|g_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{m}}\leq C(m)N_{n}^{m}.\end{split}

(6.16)

Comparison of those two estimates as
n\to\infty
leads to a contradiction, since

\begin{split}&\log(M_{n}r_{n+1}^{2d})=n^{2}\log 2-2d\log(n+1)!\sim n^{2},\\
&\log(N_{n}^{m/(n-1)})=\tfrac{mn^{2}}{n-1}\log n\sim mn\log n.\end{split}

(6.17)

Therefore,
\hat{u}_{n}(t)
is not bounded in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at any
t\not=0
.
The argument is exactly the same for the iteration
v_{n}
because of the formula (
5.4
).
∎

6.3.
Integrability in the orthogonal directions

Next we consider if
\mathcal{X}
needs the integrability condition in
\xi^{\perp}
, which is also quite strong in some sense. For example in the
L^{2}
-Sobolev spaces, we may easily observe that if
d\geq 2
then

\begin{split}\{F\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{supp}\hat{F}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}\}\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}\iff s>\tfrac{d-1}{2}.\end{split}

(6.18)

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
is bounded for each
l>0
, then we have

(\mathcal{F}H^{s})_{\mathcal{R}}\subset\mathcal{X}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

First, the necessity of (lower) bounded regularity on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<l}
for each
l>0
is observed by the simple example

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}\ni f_{N}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}c_{n}[\delta(\xi-le_{1}-ne_{2})+(-\partial_{1})^{n}\delta(\xi-le_{1}+ne_{2})]\to f_{\infty}\text{ in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0},\end{split}

(6.19)

as
N\to\infty
, where
l>0
and
\{c_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
are arbitrarily fixed.
For any
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(2le_{1},1)
, we have

\begin{split}(f_{N}*f_{N})(\varphi)=2\sum_{n=1}^{N}c_{n}^{2}(\partial_{1}^{n}\varphi)(2le_{1}),\end{split}

(6.20)

so
f_{N}*f_{N}
is not bounded in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
N\to\infty
, no matter how fast
c_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Since
\operatorname{supp}f_{N}^{*j}\subset\{\xi_{1}=jl\}
, it is easy to deduce that the solution
u_{N}
of (
5.1
) with
\hat{u}_{N}(0)=f_{N}
is also unbounded in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at any
t\not=0
,
as well as for the iteration sequence for the initial data
\hat{u}(0)=f_{\infty}
.

Second, the optimality of
L^{1}_{\xi}
may be observed from the example

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{>0}\ni g_{N}:=\chi(\xi_{1}-3){\langle\xi^{\perp}\rangle}^{-\sigma}\chi(|\xi|/N)\to g_{\infty}:=\chi(\xi_{1}-3){\langle\xi^{\perp}\rangle}^{-\sigma}\text{ in }\mathcal{B}^{\infty}\end{split}

(6.21)

as
N\to\infty
, where
\sigma\in(0,d-1)
is arbitrarily chosen.
Note that
\operatorname{supp}g_{N},\operatorname{supp}g_{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{(1,5)}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\tfrac{n-1}{n}<\tfrac{\sigma}{d-1}
, we have

\begin{split}g_{N}^{*n}\to g_{\infty}^{*n}=\chi^{*n}(\xi_{1}-3n)({\langle\xi^{\perp}\rangle}^{-\sigma})^{*n}\text{ in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq 0},\quad({\langle\xi^{\perp}\rangle}^{-\sigma})^{*n}\sim{\langle\xi^{\perp}\rangle}^{(n-1)(d-1)-n\sigma},\end{split}

(6.22)

but if
\tfrac{n-1}{n}>\tfrac{\sigma}{d-1}
then
g_{N}^{*n}
is unbounded as
N\to\infty
in
L^{1}_{loc}
and also in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
.

Thus in the same way as before, we observe the unboundedness of solutions for
\hat{u}(0)=g_{N}
and for the iteration sequence of
\hat{u}(0)=g_{\infty}
.
This means that the condition
X^{l}\hat{u}_{0}\in W^{-m,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
may not be replaced with
W^{-m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for any
p>1
, in order to keep the local wellposedness of (
5.1
).
Note also that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}g_{N}\to\mathcal{F}^{-1}g_{\infty}
converges in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
s<\sigma-\tfrac{d-1}{2}
.
Hence
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is not enough for
s<\tfrac{d-1}{2}
, even if the Fourier support is in a bounded interval of
\xi_{1}
away from
0
.
However, the above example does not preclude the endpoint case
s=\tfrac{d-1}{2}
,
for which the iterated convolution grows only logarithmically for
|\xi^{\perp}|\to\infty
.

6.4.
Cone support for unbounded coefficients

For
d\geq 2
and general smooth coefficients
\hat{L}
, the support restriction to a cone region is necessary, as the following example shows. Here we include a non-negative linear part, namely

\begin{split}u_{t}=Lu+u^{2},\quad 0\leq\hat{L}(\xi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).\end{split}

(6.23)

Suppose that
\hat{u}_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, supported and continuous on
\{|\xi^{\perp}|\leq R_{0}(\xi_{1})\}
, and positive on the interior, for some continuous
R_{0}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
R_{0}(0)=0
.

Suppose for contradiction that
R_{0}(a)/a\to+\infty
as
a\to+0
but
u
is global for
t\geq 0
.
Then the positivity of the data and the coefficients implies

\begin{split}\begin{cases}\hat{u}(t)\geq\hat{u}_{0},\\
\hat{u}\geq\hat{u}_{1}\implies\hat{u}(t)\geq\int_{0}^{t}\hat{u}_{1}(s)*\hat{u}_{1}(s)ds.\end{cases}\end{split}

(6.24)

Iteration of those inequalities yields a lower bound

\begin{split}t>0,\ \xi_{1}>0\implies\hat{u}(t,\xi)\geq\hat{u}_{*}(t,\xi)>0,\end{split}

(6.25)

for some continuous
\hat{u}_{*}
independent of
\hat{L}
.

\hat{u}_{*}
is positive everywhere on
\xi_{1}>0
because of
R_{0}(a)/a\to\infty
and
\hat{u}_{0}>0
on
|\xi|<R_{0}(\xi_{1})
.
Then we may choose
\hat{L}(\xi)
such that

\begin{split}1\leq\xi_{1}\leq 2\implies e^{\hat{L}(\xi)}\hat{u}_{*}(1,\xi)\geq 1.\end{split}

(6.26)

Then
\hat{u}(2,\xi)\geq 1
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{[1,2]}
.
Injecting it into (
6.24
) leads to blow-up of
\hat{u}
for
t>2
because
1
is not integrable for
\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

So we need
\limsup_{a\to+0}R_{0}(a)/a<\infty
to avoid such blow-up.
Then Lemma
4.4
yields
\mathcal{R}=\{|\xi|\leq R(\xi_{1})\}\supset\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}_{0}
so that the global wellposedness holds in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

7.
Global solutions with locally integrable Fourier transform

In this section, we consider solutions in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with the Fourier support in the half space, assuming that
\hat{L}
decays on the boundary.
This allows considerable simplification of both the function spaces and the construction of unique global solutions.
Note that for any
\nu\in C_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}
, the closure of
\mathscr{D}_{>0}
in
\mathcal{X}^{0,\nu}
is the weighted
L^{1}
space

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}^{0,\nu}_{0}:=\{F\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{supp}F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0},\ \nu(\xi_{1})^{-1}F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}.\end{split}

(7.1)

The solutions in this section belong to such spaces (upto rotation in
\xi
).
Note that
u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is decaying at spatial infinity in the average sense (
4.26
).

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a closed subset satisfying
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
be continuous and super-additive, namely
\mathfrak{p}(\xi+\eta)\geq\mathfrak{p}(\xi)+\mathfrak{p}(\eta)
for all
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\hat{L},\hat{M},\hat{N}:\mathcal{R}\to M(n_{1}),M(n_{2},n_{1}),M(n_{1},n_{3})
be measurable, satisfying for some

0<c_{0}\leq 1\leq C_{0}<\infty

\begin{split}\sigma_{\max}(\tfrac{\hat{L}+\hat{L}^{*}}{2})\leq(1-c_{0})\mathfrak{p},\quad\|\hat{N}\|_{M(n_{1})}+\|\hat{M}\|_{M(n_{2},n_{1})}\leq C_{0}{\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle},\end{split}

(7.2)

almost everywhere on
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
where
\sigma_{\max}
denotes the maximal eigenvalue of the self-adjoint matrix.
The above condition of
\hat{L}
implies
\|e^{t\hat{L}(\xi)}\|_{M(n_{1})}\leq e^{t(1-c_{0})\mathfrak{p}(\xi)}

a.e. on
\mathcal{R}
. Hence, all of
e^{t\hat{L}},\hat{N},\hat{M}
are locally bounded on
\mathcal{R}
.

The continuity and super-additivity of
\mathfrak{p}
may be implemented as follows. If (
7.2
) is satisfied by a locally bounded
\mathfrak{p}_{0}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
in place of
\mathfrak{p}
, such that

\begin{split}\sup\{|\xi|^{-1}\mathfrak{p}_{0}(\xi)\mid\xi\in\mathcal{R},\ |\xi|<\delta\}<\infty\end{split}

(7.3)

for some
\delta>0
, then Lemma
4.4
yields a continuous and super-additive
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
\mathfrak{p}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{p}
, hence (
7.2
) holds for this
\mathfrak{p}
.

A difference from the previous setting in
\mathcal{X}
is that
\sigma_{\max}(\tfrac{\hat{L}+\hat{L}^{*}}{2})
is required to decay as
\mathfrak{p}\to 0
, prohibiting uniform instability of
L
near
\xi_{1}=0
for
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
.
It is actually possible to allow
\hat{L}
to be merely bounded as
\mathfrak{p}\to 0
as before, but it will complicate the weight for the solution space (double-exponential in time).
Since many physical examples satisfy the decay condition, we choose the simplicity in this section.

The nonlinearity
\mathscr{H}:\mathbb{C}^{n_{2}}\to\mathbb{C}^{n_{3}}
is assumed to be holomorphic around
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{2}}
and vanishing to the order
\mathscr{H}(z)=O(z^{k_{0}+1})
for some
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, namely

\begin{split}\mathscr{H}(z)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n_{2}},\ |\alpha|\geq k_{0}+1}\tfrac{1}{\alpha!}\mathscr{H}^{(\alpha)}(0)z^{\alpha}\end{split}

(7.4)

is absolutely convergent in a
0
-neighborhood.
As
k_{0}\geq 1
increases, we may take the bigger space of initial data.
In the previous setting in
\mathcal{X}
, it was fixed to
k_{0}=1
.
Under those assumptions on
L,M,N,\mathscr{H}
, we consider the equation as before, namely

\begin{split}u=e^{tL(D)}u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)L(D)}N(D)\mathscr{H}(M(D)u(\tau))d\tau.\end{split}

(7.5)

For the initial data, we introduce a class of weighted
L^{1}
spaces.
For any
k_{0}>0>s
,
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty)
, let

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{k_{0},s}(\mathcal{R})
be the set of all Banach spaces
Z\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying for all
\varphi\in Z

(1)

\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathcal{R}
and
\||\varphi|\|_{Z}=\|\varphi\|_{Z}
.

(2)

\sup_{c\in\mathcal{R}}\|\varphi(\xi-c)\|_{Z}\leq\|\varphi\|_{Z}
.

(3)

\|W_{s,k_{0}}(\mathfrak{p})\varphi\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{R})}\leq\|\varphi\|_{Z}
,

where the decreasing function
W_{s,k_{0}}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is defined by

\begin{split}W_{s,k_{0}}(\rho):=\max(\rho^{-1/k_{0}},\rho-1/s)e^{s\rho}.\end{split}

(7.6)

W_{s,k_{0}}(\mathfrak{p})
is decreasing for
\mathfrak{p},-s,k_{0}>0
.

A typical setting is the following. Let
P:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be continuous and super-additive.
Then a maximal element of
\mathcal{Z}^{k_{0},s}_{P(\xi_{1})}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0})
is the weighted
L^{1}
on the half space:

\begin{split}&L^{k_{0},s}_{P}:=\{\varphi\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0},\ \|\varphi\|_{L^{k_{0},s}_{P}}<\infty\},\\
&\quad\|\varphi\|_{L^{k_{0},s}_{P}}:=\|W_{s,k_{0}}(P(\xi_{1}))\varphi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0})},\end{split}

(7.7)

for which the property (
2
) follows from the monotonicity of
W_{s,k_{0}}
.
Moreover,

\begin{split}1/W_{s,k_{0}}(P)\in C_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}},\quad L^{k_{0},s}_{P}=\mathcal{X}^{0,1/W_{s,k_{0}}(P)}\end{split}

(7.8)

with equivalent norms. Conversely, for any
\nu\in C^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552668}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{552845}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.11998pt}{\resizebox{622990}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.68054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}}}}_{0}
satisfying

\begin{split}\limsup_{t\to+0}t^{-1/k_{0}}\nu(t)<\infty,\end{split}

(7.9)

let
P(t):=t\sup_{0<\tau<t}\nu(\tau)^{k_{0}}/\tau
.
Then
P
is continuous and super-additive, satisfying for any
s<0
,
\nu\leq P^{1/k_{0}}\leq C_{s,k_{0}}/W_{s,k_{0}}(P)
with some constant
C_{s,k_{0}}\in(0,\infty)
depending only on
s<0<k_{0}
.
Hence
\mathcal{X}^{0,\nu}_{0}\hookrightarrow L^{k_{0},s}_{P}
.

We may also consider anisotropic
L^{p}
spaces. Let
d_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
\lambda>0
,

\begin{split}P_{\mathrm{v}}(\xi):=\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi,\quad\mathcal{R}:=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}(\mathrm{v}):|\bar{\xi}|\leq\lambda P_{\mathrm{v}}(\xi)\},\end{split}

(7.10)

where we denote
\xi=(\bar{\xi},\tilde{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-d_{0}}
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then

\begin{split}&Z:=\{\varphi\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\mathcal{R},\ \|\varphi\|_{Z}:=\|(P_{\mathrm{v}}^{-\sigma}+1)\varphi\|_{L^{r}_{\bar{\xi}}L^{1}_{\tilde{\xi}}}/C<\infty\},\end{split}

(7.11)

belongs to
\mathcal{Z}^{k_{0},s}_{P_{\mathrm{v}}}(\mathcal{R})
for any
s<0
for some
C\in(0,\infty)
, provided that
\tfrac{d_{0}}{r^{\prime}}+\sigma>\tfrac{1}{k_{0}}
.
Indeed, the property (
2
) follows from the translation invariance of
L^{r}L^{1}
together with monotonicity of
P_{\mathrm{v}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}(\mathrm{v})
, while (
3
) follows by Hölder:

\begin{split}&\|\max(P_{\mathrm{v}}^{-1/k_{0}},P_{\mathrm{v}})e^{sP_{\mathrm{v}}}\varphi\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}(\mathcal{R})}\lesssim\|1_{|\bar{\xi}|<1}|\bar{\xi}|^{\sigma-1/k_{0}}+|\bar{\xi}|e^{s|\bar{\xi}|}\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}_{\bar{\xi}}}\|(P_{\mathrm{v}}^{-\sigma}+1)\varphi\|_{L^{r}_{\bar{\xi}}L^{1}_{\tilde{\xi}}}.\end{split}

(7.12)

The following is the unique global existence in those spaces.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
d,n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{p},L,M,N,\mathscr{H}
be as above.
Then for any
s<0
,
a\geq 2
and
B>0
, there exists
b\in[1,\infty)
such that for any
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{k_{0},s}(\mathcal{R})
and any
u_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n_{1}}
satisfying
\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}_{0}\subset\mathcal{R}
and
\|e^{s\mathfrak{p}^{a}}\hat{u}_{0}\|_{Z}\leq B
,
there exists a unique global solution
u
of (
7.5
) satisfying

\begin{split}e^{b(s\mathfrak{p}^{a}-t\mathfrak{p})}\hat{u}(t)\in[Z(L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty))\cap C([0,\infty);Z)]^{n_{1}}.\end{split}

(7.13)

Proof.

We show that the equation (
7.5
) defines a contraction mapping on closed balls in the Banach space of measurable
\hat{u}:[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}
with the norm

\begin{split}\|u\|_{Z^{b,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}}:=\|e^{b(s\mathfrak{p}^{a}-t\mathfrak{p})}\hat{u}(t)\|_{Z(L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty))},\end{split}

(7.14)

if
b\geq 1
is chosen large enough.
For the initial data part,
\sigma_{\max}(\tfrac{1}{2}(\hat{L}+\hat{L}^{*}))\leq\mathfrak{p}
on
\mathcal{R}\supset\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}_{0}
implies

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{F}e^{tL}u_{0}\|_{Z^{b,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\leq\|e^{t\hat{L}}\hat{u}_{0}\|_{Z^{1,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\leq\|e^{s\mathfrak{p}^{a}}\hat{u}_{0}\|_{Z}\leq B.\end{split}

(7.15)

For the nonlinear Duhamel part, it suffices to estimate for
k\geq k_{0}

\begin{split}U(t,\xi)&:=e^{b(s\mathfrak{p}^{a}-t\mathfrak{p})}\int_{0}^{t}|e^{(t-\tau)\hat{L}}{\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle}({\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle}\hat{u}^{0})*\cdots*({\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle}\hat{u}^{k})(\tau)|d\tau\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{t}e^{(\tau-t)c_{0}b\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}G(\xi)d\tau=\tfrac{1-e^{-tc_{0}b\mathfrak{p}}}{c_{0}b}G(\xi),\end{split}

(7.16)

in terms of
U_{j}(\xi):=\|e^{b(s\mathfrak{p}^{a}-t\mathfrak{p})}\hat{u}^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty)}
, where

\begin{split}&G(\xi):=\int_{\xi=\xi^{0}+\cdots+\xi^{k}}\tfrac{{\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle}}{\mathfrak{p}}e^{bs\Omega}\prod_{0\leq j\leq k}{\langle\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{j})\rangle}U_{j}(\xi^{j})d\xi^{1}\cdots d\xi^{k},\\
&\quad\Omega:=\mathfrak{p}(\xi)^{a}-\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{j})^{a}.\end{split}

(7.17)

The above estimate by
G
used the super-additivity of
\mathfrak{p}
and the assumption on
\hat{L}
in the form
\|e^{(t-\tau)\hat{L}}\|_{M(n_{1})}\leq e^{(t-\tau)(1-c_{0})\mathfrak{p}}
and
\mathfrak{p}(\xi)\geq\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})+\cdots+\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{k})
.

In estimating
G
, by symmetry, we may assume that
\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})=\max_{0\leq j\leq k}\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{j})
.
We split this domain of
\xi^{0},\ldots,\xi^{k}
into three regions, depending on a parameter
\theta\in(0,1]
.
The super-additivity of
\mathfrak{p}
, the binomial expansion and Young imply

\begin{split}&\Omega\geq\mathfrak{p}(\xi)^{a}-\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})^{a}-\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0})^{a}\geq a\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})^{a-1}\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0}),\\
&\mathfrak{p}(\xi)\geq\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{1})+\cdots+\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{k})\geq k[\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{1})\cdots\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{k})]^{1/k}.\end{split}

(7.18)

If
\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})\leq\theta
then
\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{j})\leq\theta
for all
j
, so we just discard
\Omega\geq 0
to get

\begin{split}G|_{\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})\leq\theta}\leq{\langle\theta\rangle}^{k+2}U_{0}*V_{1}^{0}*\cdots*V_{k}^{0},\quad V_{j}^{0}:=\mathfrak{p}^{-1/k}U_{j}|_{\mathfrak{p}\leq\theta}.\end{split}

(7.19)

If
\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})\geq\theta
and
\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0})\geq\theta
, then we have
\mathfrak{p}(\xi)\geq\theta
and

\begin{split}&\Omega\geq(a-1)\theta\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})^{a-1}+\theta^{a-1}\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0})\geq\theta^{a-1}[\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})+\cdots+\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{k})],\\
&\quad G|_{\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0}),\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0})\geq\theta}\lesssim\tfrac{{\langle\theta\rangle}}{\theta}\tfrac{{\langle\sigma\rangle}}{|\sigma|}U_{0}*V_{1}^{1}*\cdots*V_{k}^{1},\quad V_{j}^{1}:={\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle}e^{\sigma\mathfrak{p}}U_{j},\end{split}

(7.20)

where
\sigma:=b\theta^{a-1}s<0
and the
\xi^{0}
dependent weight is bounded by
{\langle\rho\rangle}e^{\sigma\rho}\lesssim|\sigma|^{-1}{\langle\sigma\rangle}
.
If
\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})\geq\theta\geq\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0})
, then
\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{j})\leq\theta\leq\mathfrak{p}(\xi)
for
j=1,\ldots,k
, and

\begin{split}&\Omega\geq a\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})^{a-1}k[\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{1})\cdots\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{k})]^{1/k},\quad e^{bs\Omega}\leq(b|s|\Omega)^{-1},\\
&\quad G|_{\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})\geq\theta\geq\mathfrak{p}(\xi-\xi^{0})}\leq\tfrac{{\langle\theta\rangle}^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\tfrac{\theta}{ak|\sigma|}U_{0}*V_{1}^{0}*\cdots*V_{k}^{0}.\end{split}

(7.21)

Thus we obtain for
k\geq k_{0}

\begin{split}&G|_{\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{0})=\max_{j}\mathfrak{p}(\xi^{j})}\lesssim\tfrac{{\langle\theta\rangle}^{k+2}}{\theta}\tfrac{{\langle\sigma\rangle}}{|\sigma|}U_{0}*V_{1}^{2}*\cdots*V_{k}^{2},\quad V_{j}^{2}:=V_{j}^{0}+V_{j}^{1}.\end{split}

(7.22)

Since all
U_{j},V_{j}
are supported on
\mathcal{R}
, and
Z\in\mathcal{Z}^{k_{0},s}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, the property (
2
) of
\mathcal{Z}^{k_{0},s}_{\mathfrak{p}}
implies

\begin{split}\|U_{0}*V_{1}^{2}*\cdots*V_{k}^{2}\|_{Z}\leq\|U_{0}\|_{Z}\prod_{1\leq j\leq k}\|V_{j}^{2}\|_{L^{1}},\end{split}

(7.23)

while the property (
3
) implies for
\sigma\leq s
that
\|V_{j}^{2}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\kappa\|U_{j}\|_{Z}

with

\begin{split}\kappa:=\sup_{\rho>0}\tfrac{\rho^{-1/k}|_{\rho\leq\theta}+{\langle\rho\rangle}e^{\sigma\rho}}{\max(\rho^{-1/k_{0}},\rho-1/s)e^{s\rho}}\leq\theta^{-1/k+1/k_{0}}e^{-s\theta}+{\langle s\rangle}e^{(\sigma-s)\theta}.\end{split}

(7.24)

For any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
s<0
and
\varepsilon>0
, there are
\theta\in(0,1)
and
b\in[1,\infty)
such that
\kappa\leq\varepsilon
for all
k\geq k_{0}+1
. For
k=k_{0}
, we choose
\theta=1
. Then

\begin{split}\|U_{0}*V_{1}^{2}*\cdots*V_{k}^{2}\|_{Z}\leq\begin{cases}(2e^{-s})^{k_{0}}\prod_{0\leq j\leq k_{0}}\|U_{j}\|_{Z}&(k=k_{0})\\
\varepsilon^{k}\prod_{0\leq j\leq k}\|U_{j}\|_{Z}&(k\geq k_{0}+1).\end{cases}\end{split}

(7.25)

Injecting it into (
7.16
), (
7.17
) and (
7.22
), we may exploit the denominator
b
in (
7.16
), taking
b\geq 1
larger if necessary. Thus we obtain

Lemma 7.2
.

For any
-\infty<s<0<c_{0},\varepsilon<1
,
a\geq 2
and
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
b\in[1,\infty)
such that for any
\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{p},L
as above, any
Z\in\mathcal{Z}^{k_{0},s}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathcal{R})
, any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
and any
u^{0},\ldots,u^{k}
satisfying
\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}^{j}(t)\subset\mathcal{R}
for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\begin{split}\|U\|_{Z(L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty))}\leq\varepsilon^{k}\prod_{0\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{u}^{j}\|_{Z(L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty))},\end{split}

(7.26)

for
U
defined by (
7.16
).

The above lemma yields an estimate on the Duhamel nonlinear part

\begin{split}\|\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)L}N(D)\mathscr{H}(M(D)u)d\tau\|_{Z^{b,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\leq\sum_{|\alpha|\geq k_{0}}C_{0}\tfrac{\|\partial^{\alpha}\mathscr{H}(0)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n_{3}}}}{\alpha!}(C_{0}\varepsilon\|u\|_{Z^{b,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}})^{|\alpha|},\end{split}

(7.27)

and a similar estimate for the difference.
Hence, if we choose
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
small enough depending on
B,\mathscr{H},C_{0}
, then
the map
u\mapsto e^{tL}u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)L}\mathscr{H}(u(\tau))d\tau
is a contraction on
the closed ball
\{u\in Z^{b,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}\mid\|u\|_{Z^{b,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\leq B\}
,
and the unique global solution of (
7.5
) is obtained as its unique fixed point.

Since the Duhamel formula is continuous in
t
at each
\xi
,

u\in C([0,\infty);Z)
follows if
Z
satisfies the dominated convergence.
Otherwise, it suffices to take
b
slightly bigger.
∎

8.
Examples of the equations

We apply the above abstract results to some typical nonlinear evolution equations which come from physics, including the Navier-Stokes, the Euler, the nonlinear Klein-Gordon equations, as well as some nonlinear and nonlocal equations.
The application is straightforward, leading to new global wellposedness and unique existence results for more wild initial data than the standard settings.

8.1.
Incompressible Navier-Stokes and Euler equations

Consider the Cauchy problem for the incompressible
Navier-Stokes and Euler equations:

\displaystyle u_{t}-\varepsilon_{1}\partial_{1}^{2}u-\varepsilon_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}u+u\cdot\nabla u+\nabla p=0,\ \ {\rm div}\,u=0,

(8.1)

where
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the flow
velocity vector and
p:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{R}
describes the scalar pressure,

\Delta_{\perp}:=\partial^{2}_{2}+\cdots+\partial^{2}_{d}
, and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{\perp}\geq 0
are some constants. In the Ekman boundary layers for rotating fluids (cf.
[
12
]
), it makes sense to consider the different viscosities of the type
\varepsilon_{1}\geq 0
and
\varepsilon_{\perp}>0
. If
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{\perp}=0
, then (
8.1
) is the Euler equation.
As usual, (
8.1
) may be rewritten in the equivalent form:

\displaystyle u_{t}-\varepsilon_{1}\partial_{1}^{2}u-\varepsilon_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}u+\mathbb{P}\ \textrm{div}(u\otimes u)=0,

(8.2)

where
\mathbb{P}:=I-{\rm div}\,\Delta^{-1}\nabla
is the projection onto the divergence free vector fields,
I\in M(d)
is the identity matrix.
We need to extend the equation (
8.2
) to the complex values
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}^{d}
, since otherwise our solution space contains only the trivial
0
.
We may also extend the coefficients to
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{\perp}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Since the symbols for
L:=\varepsilon_{1}\partial_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}
and
N:=\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}
are given by

\begin{split}(e^{t\hat{L}}\hat{N})_{jk}=e^{-t\varepsilon_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}-t\varepsilon_{\perp}|\xi^{\perp}|^{2}}i(\xi_{k}-\xi_{j})\quad(j,k=1,\ldots,d),\end{split}

(8.3)

the condition
(C-s)
is satisfied with
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
and
\theta=1/2
if
\varepsilon_{\perp}>0
.
Hence, Corollary
4.2
implies that the Cauchy problem for (
8.2
) is globally wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}^{d}
for
t\geq 0
, for any
d\geq 2
,
\varepsilon_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\varepsilon_{\perp}>0
.
If both
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{\perp}>0
, then it does not depend on the direction of the half space: For any
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, it is globally wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})^{d}
on
t\geq 0
.

For more general
\varepsilon_{\perp}\in\mathbb{C}
, let
R\in C^{+}_{0}
be super-additive and
\mathcal{R}:=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d};|\xi|\leq R(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi)\}
.
Then for any
d\geq 2
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{\perp}\in\mathbb{C}
, Theorem
1.2
implies that the Cauchy problem for (
8.2
) is globally wellposed in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{v})^{d}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
The simplest choice of
\mathcal{R}
is
\{|\xi|\leq\lambda\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi\}
for some constant
\lambda>0
, but it may be much bigger as
\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi\to\infty
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}(\xi):=C[(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi)+(\mathrm{v}\cdot\xi)^{2}]
for some constant
C>0
.
If
C
is large enough, depending only on
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{\perp},\mathrm{v}
, then (
7.2
) is satisfied for some
0<c_{0}\leq 1\leq C_{0}<\infty
.
Then Theorem
7.1
implies that for any
s<0
,
a\geq 2
,
Z\in\mathcal{Z}^{1,s}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathcal{R})
, and any
e^{s\mathfrak{p}^{a}}\hat{u}_{0}\in Z
with
\operatorname{supp}\hat{u}_{0}\subset\mathcal{R}
, there exists some
b\geq 1
such that (
8.2
)
has a unique global solution
u
satisfying
e^{b(s\mathfrak{p}^{a}-t\mathfrak{p})}\hat{u}(t)\in[Z(L^{\infty}_{t}(0,\infty))\cap C([0,\infty);Z)]^{d}
.
See (
7.7
) and (
7.11
) for examples of
Z
.

For the 3D Navier-Stokes (
d=3
,
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{\perp}>0
), Li and Sinai
[
22
]
constructed complex-valued blow-up solutions with Fourier support on a ball far from the origin and inside a cone.
More precisely, they constructed a set of initial data with 10 parameters for which the energy and the enstrophy (namely the
L^{2}
and
\dot{H}^{1}
norms) of the solution blow up in finite time.
Since their initial data satisfy our conditions, it is another and highly non-trivial example of the global wellposedness beyond the classical blow-up.
Although the inductive construction of the solution works in the same way as for the simple example in Section
5.1
, the main difficulty in the Navier-Stokes equation comes from the projection
\mathbb{P}
, which eliminates the major part of the quadratic interaction under the Fourier support restriction.
Similar blow-up sets of initial data have been constructed also for the 2D Burgers equation and other hydrodynamical models
[
23
,
24
]
.

8.2.
Compressible Navier-Stokes and Euler equations

Next we consider the compressible Navier-Stokes equations for the ideal gas (cf. Lions
[
25
]
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\varrho+\operatorname{div}(\varrho u)=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\varrho u)+\operatorname{div}(\varrho u\otimes u)+\nabla p=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u:=(\alpha+\beta)\nabla\operatorname{div}u+\beta\Delta u,\\
\partial_{t}(\varrho e)+\operatorname{div}(\varrho ue)+p\operatorname{div}u-\kappa\Delta T=b_{\alpha,\beta}(u):=\tfrac{\beta}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{\top})^{2}+\alpha(\operatorname{div}u)^{2},\end{array}\right.

where
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the velocity,

\varrho:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to[0,\infty)
is the density,

T:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to[0,\infty)
is the temperature,
p=R\varrho T
is the pressure,
e=c_{v}T
is the internal energy, for some constants
\alpha,\beta,\kappa,R,c_{v}>0
.
If
\alpha=\beta=\kappa=0
, then they are the compressible Euler equations.
If
\varrho\not=0
, then the equations of momentum and energy may be rewritten as

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u+R(\nabla T+T\tfrac{\nabla\varrho}{\varrho})=\tfrac{1}{\varrho}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u,\\
&\partial_{t}T+u\cdot\nabla T+\tfrac{R}{c_{v}}T\operatorname{div}u-\tfrac{\kappa}{c_{v}\varrho}\Delta T=\tfrac{1}{c_{v}\varrho}b_{\alpha,\beta}(u).\end{split}

(8.4)

Let
\varrho=\rho_{*}+\rho
for some constant
\rho_{*}>0
. Then the system is rewritten for
(\rho,u,T)
as

\begin{split}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\rho_{*}\operatorname{div}u=-\operatorname{div}(\rho u),\\
\partial_{t}u-\tfrac{1}{\rho_{*}}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u+R\nabla T=-u\cdot\nabla u-\tfrac{R}{\rho_{*}+\rho}T\nabla\rho-\tfrac{\rho}{\rho_{*}(\rho_{*}+\rho)}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u,\\
\partial_{t}T-\tfrac{\kappa}{c_{v}\rho_{*}}\Delta T=-u\cdot\nabla T-\tfrac{R}{c_{v}}T\operatorname{div}u+\tfrac{1}{c_{v}(\rho_{*}+\rho)}b_{\alpha,\beta}(u)-\tfrac{\kappa\rho}{c_{v}\rho_{*}(\rho_{*}+\rho)}\Delta T.\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.5)

Because of the quasi-linear terms (
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u
and
\Delta T
on the right side),
the condition
(C-s)
is not satisfied as in the incompressible case, but
we may still apply Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
in the same way for global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{v})^{d}
.
Note that the singularity at
\rho=-\rho_{*}
does not matter in our setting, without any size restriction (cf. Sections
5.1
–
5.2
).
We may also take more general constants
\alpha,\beta,\kappa,R,1/c_{v}\in\mathbb{C}
.

The same argument works also for the equations of
(\rho,u,\tau)
with
T=T_{*}+\tau
for some constant
T_{*}>0
.
We may similarly consider the equations for the isentropic gas, where
p=a\varrho^{\gamma}
for some constants
a,\gamma>0
. In this case, the equations become for
(\rho,u)

\begin{split}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\rho_{*}\operatorname{div}u=-\operatorname{div}(\rho u),\\
\partial_{t}u-\tfrac{1}{\rho_{*}}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u+\tfrac{a\gamma}{\rho_{*}^{2-\gamma}}\nabla\rho=-u\cdot\nabla u+[\tfrac{a\gamma}{\rho_{*}^{2-\gamma}}-\tfrac{a\gamma}{(\rho_{*}+\rho)^{2-\gamma}}]\nabla\rho-\tfrac{\rho}{\rho_{*}(\rho_{*}+\rho)}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}u,\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.6)

to which Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
are applicable, with any constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,a\in\mathbb{C}
.

8.3.
Nonlinear wave and Klein-Gordon equations

Consider the nonlinear Klein-Gordon equation in the form

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}u_{tt}+c^{2}u-\Delta u=\mathscr{H}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}u,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{m}}u,\partial_{x}^{\beta^{1}}u_{t},\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\beta^{n}}u_{t}),\\
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\ \ u_{t}(0,x)=u_{1}(x),\end{array}\right.

for
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}
, with a constant
c\geq 0
,
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha^{1},\ldots,\alpha^{m},\beta^{1},\ldots,\beta^{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
and
\mathscr{H}:\mathbb{C}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{C}
defined and holomorphic in a neighborhood of
0
, satisfying
\mathscr{H}(0)=0
.
It is the nonlinear wave equation if
c=0
.
The equation may be expanded into a first order system for
U=(U_{0},\ldots,U_{d+1}):=(u,\nabla u,u_{t})

\begin{split}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}U_{0}-U_{d+1}=0,\\
\partial_{t}U_{k}-\partial_{k}U_{d+1}=0\quad(1\leq k\leq d),\\
\partial_{t}U_{d+1}+c^{2}U_{0}-\sum_{1\leq k\leq d}\partial_{k}U_{k}=\tilde{\mathscr{H}}(U),\\
U(0)=(u_{0},\nabla u_{0},u_{1}),\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.9)

where
\tilde{\mathscr{H}}(U)
is an analytic function of derivatives of
U
derived from
\mathscr{H}(\partial^{\alpha^{1}}_{x}u,\ldots,\partial^{\beta^{n}}_{x}u_{t})
, by replacing
u_{t}
with
U_{d+1}
and
\partial^{\alpha}_{x}u
with
\partial^{\alpha-e_{k}}_{x}U_{k}
(if
\alpha\geq e_{k}
) or with
U_{0}
(if
\alpha=0
).
The linear part of
\tilde{\mathscr{H}}
should be included in the operator
L
in applying our results.

For any solution
U
of the above system, the equations imply
U_{d+1}=\partial_{t}U_{0}
and
U_{k}=\nabla U_{0}+\varphi
for some
\varphi(x)
independent of
t
. Hence the initial constraint
U_{k}(0)=\nabla U_{0}(0)
implies
U_{k}=\nabla U_{0}
for all time. Then by definition of
\tilde{\mathscr{H}}
,
U_{0}
satisfies the original equation (
8.3
). The converse relation is obvious from the definition of the system.

If the equation is semilinear in
\xi^{\perp}
, namely
d=1
or

\begin{split}\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\sum_{2\leq k\leq d}\alpha^{j}_{k}\leq 1,\quad\max_{1\leq j\leq n}\sum_{2\leq k\leq d}\beta^{j}_{k}=0,\end{split}

(8.10)

then
\tilde{\mathscr{H}}
has no derivative in
\xi^{\perp}
, while
e^{t\hat{L}}
is bounded on
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, including derivatives, locally in
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Hence the condition
(C-s)
is satisfied with
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
and
\theta=0
,
and Corollary
4.2
implies the global wellposedness of the system for
U\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}^{d+2}
on
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
|\alpha^{j}|\leq 1
and
\beta^{j}=0
for all
j
, then it is independent of the direction of the half space: the global wellposedness holds in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})^{d+1}
for any
