\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.

In the general case of arbitrary derivatives
\{\alpha^{j},\beta^{j}\}
, we may apply Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
to the above system, which imply global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathrm{v})^{d+2}
and unique global existence in weighted
Z
with frequencies in the conical region
\mathcal{R}
as before.

8.4.
Nonlinear Schrödinger equations

Similarly to the Klein-Gordon equations, we may consider the nonlinear Schrödinger equations in the form

\begin{split}iu_{t}+\Delta u=\mathscr{H}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}u,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{m}}u),\end{split}

(8.11)

for
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}
with the same type of nonlinearity as above.
In this case, the global wellposedness by Corollary
4.2
works if there is no derivative in the orthogonal directions, namely
\alpha_{k}^{j}=0
for all
j
and
k\geq 2
.
Otherwise, we may still apply Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
for the global wellposedness on the conical region.

Note that the standard nonlinear Schrödinger equations are not in the above form, because of the complex conjugate. However, we may consider the equations in the form

\begin{split}iu_{t}+\Delta u=\mathscr{H}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}u,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{m}}u,\partial_{x}^{\beta^{1}}u^{\star},\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\beta^{n}}u^{\star}),\end{split}

(8.12)

where
u^{\star}
denotes the complex conjugate in the Fourier space, namely

\begin{split}u^{\star}(t,x):=\overline{u(t,-x)},\quad\mathcal{F}u^{\star}(t,\xi)=\overline{\mathcal{F}u(t,\xi)}.\end{split}

(8.13)

See
[
1
,
2
]
for the one-dimensional integrable models, with cubic terms containing one
u^{\star}
and at most one derivative.
Unique global solutions were constructed in
[
7
]
under the Fourier support restriction in
(0,\infty)
.

The above equation may be expanded to the system of
U=(u,v):=(u,u^{\star})
with

\begin{split}\begin{cases}iu_{t}+\Delta u=\mathscr{H}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}u,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{m}}u,\partial_{x}^{\beta^{1}}v,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\beta^{n}}v),\\
iv_{t}-\Delta v=-\bar{\mathscr{H}}((-\partial_{x})^{\alpha^{1}}v,\ldots,(-\partial_{x})^{\alpha^{m}}v,(-\partial_{x})^{\beta_{1}}u,\ldots,(-\partial_{x})^{\beta^{n}}u),\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.14)

where
\bar{\mathscr{H}}
is the power series derived from
\mathscr{H}
by replacing all the coefficients with their complex conjugate.
v=u^{\star}
is an invariant subspace of this system.

We may apply Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
to get global solutions to this system.
If it has no derivative in the orthogonal directions, then Corollary
4.2
is also applicable for the global wellposedness on the half space.
The invariance of
v=u^{\star}
is immediate from the uniqueness in those results.
In particular, the simplest model from
[
1
]
:

\begin{split}i\dot{u}+u_{xx}=\alpha u^{2}u^{\star},\end{split}

(8.15)

for
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
and a constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
,
is globally wellposed for
t\in\mathbb{R}
in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
,
as well as in the smaller spaces
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}_{\geq\varepsilon}
, for all
\varepsilon>0
.

If
\alpha\not=0
, this equation has a family of stationary solutions

\begin{split}u(x)=\tfrac{c}{x+iz}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[-ic1_{\xi>0}e^{-z\xi}],\quad c:=(2/\alpha)^{1/2},\quad z\in(0,\infty),\end{split}

(8.16)

contained in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}\cap H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, as well as in (
7.7
) and (
7.11
) with
\mathrm{v}=1
.
The solutions
\tfrac{c}{x+iz}
with
z<0
are in those spaces with
\mathrm{v}=-1
,
while the spaces with
\mathrm{v}=1
contain more exotic solutions:
\tfrac{c}{x+i0}=c(p.v.\tfrac{1}{x}-i\pi\delta)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[-ic1_{\xi>0}]\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[-ic1_{\xi>0}e^{z\xi}]\not\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
with
z>0
.

One may wonder if the same argument would work for the standard equations with
\bar{u}
in the nonlinearity. In fact the global results apply to the extended system of
(u,v):=(u,\bar{u})
,
but the intersection of the invariant subspace
v=\bar{u}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
is only the trivial
0
solution.

8.5.
Completely integrable equations

For nonlinear dispersive equations, it is known that complete integrability may drastically lower the regularity needed for the Cauchy problem to be wellposed.
In particular, for the KdV equation

\begin{split}u_{t}+u_{xxx}=6uu_{x}\end{split}

(8.17)

for
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+1}\to\mathbb{R}
, Kappeler and Topalov
[
18
]
proved the global wellposedness in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, and Killip and Vişan
[
20
]
in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{R})
.
On the other hand, Molinet
[
27
,
28
]
proved the illposedness in
H^{s}
for
s<-1
on both the domains.

For mixed initial data, Tsugawa
[
33
]
proved the local wellposedness in a Banach space of quasi-periodic functions, including initial data in the form

\begin{split}u_{0}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{k},\quad f_{k}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}/\ell_{k}\mathbb{Z}),\quad s>-\tfrac{1}{2N},\end{split}

(8.18)

for any fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{\ell_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq N}\subset(0,\infty)
. For almost periodic data (which forms a closed subspace in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
), the unique global solution was constructed by Binder, Damanik, Goldstein and Lukic
[
4
]
under some spectral conditions. Laurens
[
21
]
proved the global wellposedness in
V(t)+H^{-1}(\mathbb{R})
, around any fixed solution
V\in C(\mathbb{R};H^{5}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))
.

For the complex-valued solutions, Birnir
[
5
]
proved blow-up in
L^{\infty}_{x}
of the solutions given by Airault, McKean and Moser
[
3
]
, for the initial data

u(0,x)=6\wp(x+is)
,
where
\wp
is the Weierstrass elliptic function with the periods
1,i\omega
for some
\omega>0
, and
s\in(-\omega,\omega)
. The Fourier support is not within
[0,\infty)
.
The solution is in the form

\begin{split}u(t,x)=2[\wp(x+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z(t)+is)+\wp(x-z(t)+is)+\wp(x-\bar{z}(t)+is)]\end{split}

(8.19)

for some smooth
z:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying
z(0)=0
,
and the blow-up occurs when
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)\in\pm s+\omega\mathbb{Z}
due to the poles of
\wp
.
Similarly to Remark
5.2
, such singularities are not continuous in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.

On the other hand, Corollary
4.2
yields the global wellposedness for
t\in\mathbb{R}
in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
, where more wild initial data are allowed, under the Fourier support constraint. Indeed,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
includes all initial data in the form

\begin{split}u_{0}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{n},\quad\begin{cases}f_{0}\in H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}),\ \operatorname{supp}\mathcal{F}f_{0}\subset[0,\infty),&(n=0),\\
f_{n}\in H^{s_{n}}(\mathbb{R}/\ell_{n}\mathbb{Z}),\ \operatorname{supp}\mathcal{F}f_{n}\subset(0,\infty)&(n\not=0),\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.20)

for any sequences
\{s_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}
and
\{\ell_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset(0,\infty)
, satisfying

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}\ell_{n}=0,\quad\inf_{\delta>0}\sum_{n<0}\|\mathcal{F}f_{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{<\delta})}<\infty.\end{split}

(8.21)

Theorem
7.1
also yields unique global solutions.

Similarly to the previous equation (
8.15
), the KdV has stationary solutions

\begin{split}u(x)=\tfrac{1}{(x+iz)^{2}}=-\mathcal{F}^{-1}[1_{\xi>0}\xi e^{-z\xi}]\quad(z\in\mathbb{C},\ \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z>0)\end{split}

(8.22)

contained in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}\cap H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and other spaces.
The solutions
\tfrac{1}{(x+iz)^{2}}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z<0
are in the spaces with
\mathrm{v}=-1
, while the spaces with
\mathrm{v}=1
contain also
\tfrac{1}{(x-a+i0)^{2}}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
for
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[1_{\xi>0}\xi e^{-z\xi}]\not\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
for
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z<0
.

The same story holds for some other integrable equations, such as the modified KdV equation and the Benjamin-Ono equation. Since our wellposedness results require little for the equations, they
are convenient for equations derived from algebraic viewpoints.
For example, consider the deformed AKNS system on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}u_{t}-u_{xx}=2u^{2}v+u^{2}v(vu_{yy}+2u_{y}v_{y})+2u(vu_{xy}+v_{x}u_{y}),\\
v_{t}+v_{xx}=-2v^{2}u-v^{2}u(uv_{yy}+2u_{y}v_{y})-2v(uv_{xy}+u_{x}v_{y}),\\
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\ \ v(0,x)=v_{0}(x),\end{array}\right.

for
(u,v)(t,x,y):\mathbb{R}^{1+2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
, where the second equation is a backward parabolic equation. (
8.5
) is Lax integrable and symmetry integrable, cf.
[
26
]
.
Theorems
1.2
and
7.1

apply to the above system as well.
Similarly, we may apply those results to the integrable
(3+1)
-dimensional KdV equation in
[
17
]
:

\begin{split}&u_{t}-u_{xxx}=3bu_{z}(u_{x}+a_{1}uu_{y}+a_{2}u^{2}u_{z})^{2}\\
&\qquad+\partial_{x}(-2u^{3}+\tfrac{3}{2}a^{2}u^{2}u_{yy}+3auu_{xy}+3bu^{2}u_{xz}+\tfrac{3}{2}b^{2}u^{4}u_{zz}+3abu^{3}u_{zy})\\
&\qquad+a\partial_{y}(a^{2}u^{3}u_{yy}-u^{4}+\tfrac{3}{2}au^{2}u_{xy}+\tfrac{9}{4}abu^{4}u_{zy}+\tfrac{6}{5}b^{2}u^{5}u_{zz}+2bu^{3}u_{xz})\\
&\qquad+b\partial_{z}(b^{2}u^{6}u_{zz}-\tfrac{3}{5}au^{5}+\tfrac{3}{2}bu^{4}u_{xz}+\tfrac{3}{4}a^{2}u^{4}u_{yy}+\tfrac{9}{5}abu^{5}u_{zy}+au^{3}u_{xy}),\end{split}

(8.26)

where
u(t,x,y,z):\mathbb{R}^{1+3}\to\mathbb{C}
and
a,b,a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
are some constants.

8.6.
Nonlinear hyperbolic conservation laws

The general systems of nonlinear hyperbolic conservation laws on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{j}+\sum^{d}_{k=1}\partial_{k}\mathscr{H}_{jk}(u)=0\quad(j=1,\ldots,n),\ \

(8.27)

for
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
, may be also treated by our results, if each
\mathscr{H}_{jk}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
is defined and analytic in some neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Decomposing
\mathscr{H}
into the linear and super-linear parts, one may easily see that Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
apply to the above system.
In fact, it does not matter whether the nonlinearity is in the divergence form.

8.7.
Nonlinear parabolic equations

Constantin–Lax–Majda
[
11
,
32
]
proposed the following one-dimensional model of the vorticity equation:

\displaystyle v_{t}=\varepsilon v_{xx}+v\mathcal{H}v,

(8.28)

where
v:\mathbb{R}^{1+1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(i\operatorname{sign}\xi)\mathcal{F}
is the Hilbert transform.
In the same way as for the KdV, Corollary
4.2
yields the global wellposedness in
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
, and Theorem
7.1
yields unique global solutions, both for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{C}
.
It has also the stationary solutions in the same form,
v(x)=-c\mathcal{F}^{-1}[1_{\xi>0}\xi e^{-z\xi}]
with
c:=6i\varepsilon
.

More generally, we may consider the fractional heat equation

\displaystyle u_{t}=\varepsilon(-\Delta)^{s/2}u+\mathscr{H}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}u,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{m}}u),

(8.29)

for
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}
, where
\varepsilon,s\in\mathbb{C}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha^{1},\ldots,\alpha^{m}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
are constants and
\mathscr{H}:\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathbb{C}
is holomorphic in a
0
-neighborhood, satisfying
\mathscr{H}(0)=0
.
Here the boundedness of
L=(-\Delta)^{s/2}
as
\xi_{1}\to 0
requires
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}s\geq 0
.
If
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\varepsilon>0
and

\begin{split}\bar{\alpha}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\sum_{2\leq k\leq d}\alpha_{k}^{j}<\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}s,\end{split}

(8.30)

then the condition
(C-s)
with
\theta=\bar{\alpha}/\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}s
and
\mathrm{v}=e_{1}
is satisfied by the equation of
{\langle\nabla\rangle}^{\bar{\alpha}}u
, so Corollary
4.2
yields the global wellposedness for
u\in{\langle\nabla\rangle}^{-\bar{\alpha}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}
on
t\geq 0
.
If
\tilde{\alpha}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|\alpha^{j}|<\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}s
, then it is independent of the direction: the global wellposedness holds for
u\in{\langle\nabla\rangle}^{-\tilde{\alpha}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{v})
on
t\geq 0
for any
\mathrm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
Theorem
1.2
is applicable without any restriction on
\{\alpha^{j}\}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{C}
for
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}s\geq 0
.
If
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}s\geq 1
, then the decay condition of
\hat{L}
in (
7.2
) is satisfied, so that Theorem
7.1
can also be applied.

We may also consider nonlinear diffusion, such as the porous medium equation:

\begin{split}&u_{t}=\Delta(u^{m}),\end{split}

(8.31)

or the fast diffusion equation, and the
p
-Laplace heat equation:

\begin{split}u_{t}=\operatorname{div}(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u),\quad|\nabla u|^{p-2}:=[\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\partial_{j}u)^{2}]^{p/2-1}.\end{split}

(8.32)

Here
u(t,x):\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
m,p>0
are given constants.

In fact, if
m\geq 2
is an even integer, or
p\geq 4
is an even integer, then the right side is already in the nonlinear form of our setting, after extending
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}
,
so that we may apply Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
.

In the remaining case of
m
or
p
, we may expand the power around a fixed constant.
For the porous medium, let
u=u_{*}+v
for some constant
u_{*}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then the equation is rewritten for
v
as

\begin{split}v_{t}-mu_{*}^{m-1}\Delta v=\Delta\mathscr{H}_{m}^{u_{*}}(v),\quad\mathscr{H}_{m}^{u_{*}}(v):=\sum_{n\geq 2}\tfrac{\Gamma(m+1)u_{*}^{m-n}}{\Gamma(m-n+1)n!}v^{n},\end{split}

(8.33)

which is in the form to which Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
apply.
For the
p
-Laplacian, let
u=u_{*}+\xi_{*}\cdot x+v
for some constants
u_{*}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\xi_{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Then the equation is rewritten for
v

\begin{split}&v_{t}-|\xi_{*}|^{p-2}\Delta v-(p-2)|\xi_{*}|^{p-4}\operatorname{div}(\xi_{*}\cdot\nabla v)=\operatorname{div}\mathscr{H}_{p,\xi_{*}}(\nabla v),\\
&\quad\mathscr{H}_{p,\xi_{*}}(V):=\mathscr{H}_{p/2-1}^{|\xi_{*}|^{2}}(2\xi_{*}\cdot V+|V|^{2})(\xi_{*}+V)+(p-2)|\xi_{*}|^{p-4}(\xi_{*}\cdot V)V,\end{split}

(8.34)

to which we may apply Theorems
1.2
and
7.1
.
Of course, we may consider various combinations of those nonlinear terms.
